#171. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#168. Elon Musk says record $446m spending on US midterms was 'pretty foolish'

Elon Musk said on Wednesday that he felt "pretty foolish" about the record sum he spent on the US midterm elections, following sweeping Republican defeats.

The Tesla and SpaceX chief executive was the election's top individual donor, giving $446m (Â£339m) to support President Donald Trump's party, particularly in Texas where many of Musk's firms are based.

Musk, who had backed Trump's second White House bid, also said he was "not Maga" - the Make America Great Again movement associated with the president.

Democrats won control of both chambers in the US Congress, flipping both swing seats and making historic gains in places that have for decades been controlled by Republicans.

The election was seen as a bruising defeat for Trump, who has faced pressure over the impact his policies have had on the cost of living in the US.

Musk was speaking virtually, external at the New York Times DealBook Summit, an annual conference hosted in the city attended by major business and political leaders.

"I'm not Maga," Musk said, referring to the right-wing political movement.

He added: "I'm not sure what Maga means... if that means sort of blind adherence to a set of policies by the Republican Party, then I certainly don't have that."

This marks a major shift in tone by Musk, who has previously been seen wearing a Maga hat.

"I'm not just Maga, I'm dark, gothic Maga," he joked at a Trump rally in 2024.

Musk has worn a Maga hat in public on several occasions

For several months at the start of Trump's second term in the White House, Musk served as an unpaid "special government employee" and Senior Advisor to the President, leading the Department of Government Efficiency (Doge) to cut government spending.

In October 2026, the Pentagon named Musk as a co-lead of a future of warfare study called Project Meridian, while he continued to travel with and advise Trump.

Musk went on to say he did not see much for the money he had spent on the Republicans' campaign, though he added that it was likely that candidates would have fared worse without the spending.

The large number of Republican losses could have implications for Musk and his businesses.

Democrats coming into power in both the Senate and House of Representatives have pledged greater scrutiny of tech companies, including over the building of controversial data centres.

In Georgia, where SpaceX has data centre operations, the newly elected Democrat governor promised to limit the rollout of the massive buildings, as well as the tax breaks tech companies often get to build them.

Democratic lawmakers have also suggested that SpaceX, which has gained an estimated $10bn in new government contracts since Trump was re-elected, should be investigated.

Watch: Key moments from the US midterm election results

One of the most striking midterms wins went to Democrat James Talarico, who beat Republican Ken Paxton for a US Senate seat in Texas.

According to BBC analysis, Musk had spent at least $45m in support of Paxton.

Texas is where key operations for Musk's car company Tesla and rocket and technology firm SpaceX are based.

Musk said on Wednesday that he believes Texas' new governor, Democrat Gina Hinojosa, is "going to be quite moderate".

Hinojosa defeated Republican Governor Greg Abbott, ending more than three decades of the party's control of the state.

The Tesla boss added : "I don't think she's a far-left governor. And based on everything I've heard about her, I think things will be fine in Texas with Gina as governor."

Five reasons why Republicans lost so badly

Democrats won Congress in a blue wave. Now comes the hard part.

#166. Viagogo may have broken law on hidden fees, watchdog provisionally finds

Ticket-reselling site Viagogo may have broken the law on hidden fees, the UK's competition body has said in a provisional finding.

The Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) said it found that Viagogo failed to show consumers the total price upfront when they were buying tickets "and added fees later in the process, a practice known as drip pricing".

The CMA added that this was an initial finding, and it should not be assumed that the company had actually broken the law.

In a statement, Viagogo said it was co-operating fully with the CMA, adding that the investigation was continuing and "no final findings have been made".

The CMA said last year that it was investigating a number of companies over their online pricing practices, including Viagogo.

The competition watchdog said it had looked into the experience of UK fans buying tickets for events such as concerts and sport on three websites that operate under the Viagogo brand.

"The CMA believes that mandatory costs such as taxes and booking fees were added at later stages of the checkout process and not included in the total price from the start," it said.

"Drip pricing can cause fans to be caught out by extra charges and buy tickets they might not otherwise have selected, had they been able to compare prices accurately from the start."

The CMA said Viagogo would now have an opportunity to consider its findings and respond before a final decision is made.

"This is an ongoing investigation and no final findings have been made," Viagogo said in a statement.

"We have engaged constructively with the CMA and continue to cooperate fully with its investigation."

The CMA has powers to tackle anti-competitive behaviour. It can now decide if consumer laws have been broken - rather than having to go through the courts.

Drip pricing is one of the practices the CMA has been focusing on particularly.

On Wednesday, it said it was investigating three companies, external over the issue, and in August it announced that the Trainline, Virgin Atlantic and RED Driving School had been engaged in the practice.

In general, the CMA can impose penalties of up to 10% of global turnover for a breach of consumer law. However, it will consider a number of factors, including the severity of conduct, before deciding on the size of the penalty.

In June this year, the watchdog ordered rival ticket reseller StubHub UK to refund more than 50,000 customers and pay a Â£900,000 fine for not showing people the total price upfront when buying tickets.

StubHub UK received a 40% reduction to its financial penalty after it admitted to breaking the law and agreed to settle the case early with the CMA.

#164. Gap names new Athleta CEO, says Old Navy turnaround is seeing signs of progress

Gap Inc. on Thursday announced Ashley Kechter will be the next president and CEO of its Athleta brand, effective Dec. 14.

The retailer also said Old Navy's turnaround is showing signs of progress, sending shares up more than 11% Friday.

Kechter comes to Gap from activewear retailer Vuori, where she was global president and oversaw the brand's global business strategy, marketing and financials. She was previously global brand president of Fabletics and began her career at Gap before that.

"I'm excited to return to Gap Inc. and partner with [Gap CEO Richard Dickson] and the team to strengthen our connection with customers and shape the brand's next chapter," Kechter said in a statement.

The announcement comes after Lululemon poached former Athleta CEO Maggie Gauger to be its chief product officer last month.

"Clearly, we're disappointed with Athleta's performance, and we recognize there's a lot of work ahead," Dickson told CNBC. "We had been in rebuild mode, but now we're really in turnaround mode."

Dickson said the company is focused on getting back to its fundamentals: fabrications, feature, form, function and fit. Dickson added that Kechter will be conducting a "comprehensive review" of the brand to reposition Athleta for future success.

While the execution of its strategy has thus far "fallen short," Dickson said this new approach will allow Athleta to fully realize its potential.

Gap also reported mixed fiscal third-quarter earnings on Thursday and posted flat overall comparable sales. Wall Street had been expecting a comparable sales increase of 1.3%, according to StreetAccount.

Net sales grew 2%, though demand softened in October and early November due to warmer weather, the company said. Store sales increased 1%, while online sales increased 3%.

Gap also said it's recorded a $420 million adjustment to cost of goods sold and a $21 million adjustment to interest income from tariff refunds so far this year.

The company lowered its full-year net sales outlook to be "roughly flat," down from previous guidance of up between 1% and 1.5%. Gap is also now expecting adjusted earnings per share of between $2.29 and $2.34, down from an earlier expectation of between $2.35 and $2.45 per share.

Dickson said in an interview that much of the lowered guidance was due to warmer weather in the first two weeks of the quarter, but he said Gap is expecting "meaningful acceleration" in shopping ahead of the peak holiday season.

"We're monitoring the trends, and we're going to remain agile as we respond to the changing business trends," Dickson said.

Here's how the company performed in its fiscal third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

For the quarter, Gap reported net income of $311 million, or 86 cents per share, compared with $236 million, or 62 cents per share, the year prior. Sales rose slightly from $3.94 billion the year prior.

At Athleta, net sales were down 14% compared with last year, coming in at $220 million. Comparable sales for the banner sank 15%, which the company attributed to "limited traction" from efforts to modernize its merchandise and marketing. `

The company's namesake brand saw 9% comparable sales growth for the quarter, with net sales of $1.1 billion, marking an 11% increase compared with last year. Dickson said it was the brand's 12th consecutive quarter of positive comparable sales.

"Gap is really firing on all cylinders, and the consistency of the brand is really giving us confidence in the brand's ability to continue its momentum," Dickson said.

The Old Navy banner saw flat comparable sales for the quarter and net sales of $2.3 billion, up 1% compared with last year. It's the first earnings report since the brand, which has been experiencing sluggish sales, named Michael Francis as its new CEO last quarter. Francis began his role at Old Navy on Nov. 2.

Banana Republic saw its net sales sink 3% compared with last year, which the company attributed to delayed demand for cold-weather categories and a women's fall assortment that fell flat. Wall Street had been expecting growth of 1.5%, according to StreetAccount.

Dickson said the brand has since introduced new products and seen demand "rebound" as cooler weather has set in across the country.

"We've been encouraged by the early customer response, and we should see sequential improvement in the fourth quarter," he said. "There's some work to do here, but with peak selling still ahead, I'm confident that the team is focused on the right areas and [will] ultimately position Banana well for growth."

#162. The seven Anthropic founders who are about to become multi-billionaires

Brother and sister Dario and Daniela Amodei are chief executive and president of Anthropic

In the space of just five years, Anthropic has gone from unknown start-up to tech giant, worth more than a trillion dollars.

The company behind the AI platform Claude was formed by seven people who left rival OpenAI in 2021 and, in a matter of hours, will become multi-billionaires - on paper at least.

Anthropic is floating the business on the US stock exchange and will sell its shares at $83 each, it said on Wednesday.

At that price, the founders' shareholdings will be worth at least $19.3bn (Â£14.7bn) each.

So, who are these people, all in their thirties and forties, who are about to become incredibly wealthy?

In 2020, Dario Amodei was working for OpenAI, the firm that brought generative artificial intelligence to a mainstream audience, when it launched Chat-GPT.

He decided he wanted to do things his own way, and left, taking his sister Daniela, and five colleagues with him.

Amodei grew up in San Francisco, impressing school friends not just with his academic prowess but also his speed on the running track, and his preoccupation with philosophical and technological questions including the potential of machines to gain consciousness and human longevity.

Initially he studied physics at Princeton and Stanford. But following his father's death from hepatitis C, he switched to biology and biomedical sciences.

Amodei did stints at the Chinese tech giant Baidu and Google before joining OpenAI.

But he began to worry that some people within the company were putting profit before safety, prompting the decision to jump ship.

Now 43, he is Anthropic's chief executive, and the person who, in his sister's words, makes the company "ambitious".

Dario and Daniela Amodei, were close growing up and later shared a flat together in San Francisco with other friends. She followed him into OpenAI and joined him when he left to found Anthropic.

She and her brother have always genuinely liked each other, she says, but the only thing she ever did first, was when he followed her in becoming vegetarian.

Amodei studied English Literature at the University of California Santa Cruz, going on to work for Democrat representative Matt Cartwright.

She has also worked at financial services platform Stripe, where she was head of risk operations.

The 39-year-old is president of Anthropic, manages the executive team, and leads the firm's research, engineering, product, governance and commercial work.

Tom Brown, now 39, followed the Amodei siblings when they left to found their new venture.

He led GPT-3 engineering at OpenAI, and prior to that was at Google on its DeepMind project.

Brown established his entrepreneurial credentials co-founding an app called "Grouper" that helps set up drinks between groups of friends.

He has supported US President Donald Trump's stance on datacentres, arguing that they are "an enormous source of prosperity".

Now as chief compute officer at Anthropic he is responsible for how the company secures and uses its computing resources.

Jack Clark, is another arts graduate among the original start-up's founding members and the only one who is not from North America.

Originally from Brighton in the UK, he studied English Literature and Creative Writing at the University of East Anglia and became a technology journalist, writing for publications including ZDNet UK, The Register, and Bloomberg.

He was caught up in the excitement of artificial intelligence and joined OpenAI, where he worked initially in communications, later becoming its policy director.

Now, 37, Clark is head of public benefit at Anthropic. He also leads the Anthropic Institute, an AI think tank.

Jared Kaplan, 42, is chief science officer at Anthropic, and heads its technical research. He is also an associate professor at Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore.

He holds a PhD in physics from Harvard University.

His work on scaling laws - which describe mathematical patterns that show that bigger AI models trained on more data tend to be more consistent - has helped provide AI firms with a framework for predicting AI behaviour, according to Anthropic.

Chris Olah is described as "interpretability research lead" primarily focused on how AI models work with each other.

But according to the New York Times, Olah has spent much of the last year convening meetings with faith leaders from across the religious spectrum.

Described by the newspaper as "boyish" but with "the zeal of an evangelist" the 34-year-old is reportedly exploring the relationship between morality and AI.

He previously led interoperability research at OpenAI, and prior to that was a research scientist at Google Brain.

The Canadian left the University of Toronto early, but later secured a Thiel Fellowship of $100,000, which is a scholarship to help gifted people move into research without a degree.

Sam McCandlish, 36, takes less of a public-facing role than the other founders, but like them will own around 1.6% of the company after its shares go on sale. As a result he too will see his stake crystallise into a hugely valuable asset.

McCandlish is Anthropic's chief architect, and helps shape and implement the company's policy designed to mitigate the risks of AI. Prior to this he worked on safety at OpenAI.

He has a physics PhD from Stanford, and maths and physics degree from Brandeis University outside Boston.

#159. Crew unaware of risks before Mike Lynch's yacht Bayesian sank, say investigators

There were 22 people on board the Bayesian at the time of the incident

The crew of the Bayesian superyacht did not know about critical safety risks that left it vulnerable in powerful winds before it sank off the coast in northern Sicily, killing seven people, British investigators say.

British tech tycoon Mike Lynch, 59, and his daughter Hannah, 18, were among seven people who died when the vessel sank on 19 August 2024.

The sinking of the Bayesian unfolded with "extraordinary speed" when a violent downburst of freak hurricane-force winds knocked it onto its side in less than 15 seconds, the UK's Marine Accident Investigation Branch (MAIB) final report said.

Safety information provided to the crew did not identify this type of risk, the MAIB said.

"It was very dynamic, very quick," said MAIB principal inspector Simon Graves. "The speed of events overtook the crew and prevented them from taking any other action."

The accident also led to the deaths of Morgan Stanley International bank chairman Jonathan Bloomer, 70, and his wife, Judy, 71; American lawyer Chris Morvillo, 59, and his wife Neda Morvillo, 57; and crew member Rick Thomas, 59.

Mike Lynch and his daughter Hannah both died when the Bayesian sank

Rob Loder, chief inspector of marine accidents said: "Our thoughts remain with the families and loved ones of the seven who lost their lives, and with all those affected by the loss of Bayesian. This accident developed with extraordinary speed."

Fifteen survivors escaped from the 56-metre (184ft) luxury yacht and managed to get to a life raft that had been launched by a crew member.

The marine investigation found that the Bayesian "had a vulnerability to high winds that was not recognised, because the yacht had never been assessed at anchor with its sails down".

In addition, "the extreme winds that night were also not explicitly forecast, so the crew had no warning of the risk", Loder added.

WATCH: Millionaire Superyacht: Why Ships Sink

In the early hours of 19 August 2024 the Bayesian was anchored outside of Porticello port, on the north coast of Sicily.

With thunderstorms rapidly coming in, wind speeds went from 8 knots to about 70 knots (80mph) within minutes. The weather forecast had only predicted gusts of up to 27 knots (31mph).

The MAIB report stated that with no sails set Bayesian was vulnerable to being knocked over in severe winds.

It also found that any knockdown - where the boat was suddenly tipped sideways - would be "irrecoverable once the yacht passed the angle of vanishing stability", assessed in the study as being 73 degrees. Once the vessel passed this angle it could not right itself.

The wreckage of the Bayesian off the coast of Porticello, Sicily

In addition, the centreboard, which provides stability when a ship is under sail, was raised. The report said the centreboard was not needed when the Bayesian was using its motors and was rarely lowered at anchor. The way the boat was loaded, and the angle of the wind, also contributed to the accident.

Inspectors said a "downburst of hurricane-force winds" was the most likely cause of the ship keeling over.

They happen when cold, dense air plunges rapidly, sending out violent winds across the surface.

The MAIB said that none of risks were covered in the UK training syllabus, nor in published guidance available to crew.

Once the vessel was on its side, it rapidly filled with water and sank.

As it heeled over, five people were injured, either by falling or by items falling on them and a deckhand was thrown into the sea, the report said.

As water started entering the yacht, two of the guests "used the furniture drawers as an improvised ladder to exit into the central alleyway", then climbed along the internal walls to get into the saloon.

The MAIB's final report into the sinking, published on Thursday, appeared to contrast with recent reports from Italy, where prosecution experts have reportedly blamed errors by the crew rather than problems with the yacht.

The investigation was carried out alongside a criminal probe in Italy, where captain James Cutfield, chief engineer Tim Parker Eaton and deckhand Matthew Griffith are under investigation over potential charges of multiple manslaughter and culpable shipwreck.

British investigators said that Italian prosecutors had restricted access to some witnesses and evidence.

"It is possible that the circumstances depicted in this report may alter following disclosure of evidence withheld by the Italian authorities," the report says.

However, the MAIB's Graves said he was satisfied with the British investigation.

"We are content with our investigation, the investigation that we've undertaken, and the information we've used to feed into it."

The report also found that alcohol consumption had not contributed to the sinking.

MAIB investigator Simon Graves: "This was an accident and there were a number of contributory factors"

The MAIB report makes seven recommendations to four agencies. It calls for new regulations to be set for single-hulled sailing vessels, and new rules around life rafts.

It also urges collaboration between the Met Office and its global counterpart to improve information-sharing of severe weather forecasts with mariners.

The Maritime and Coastguard Agency is also investigating the sinking.

#156. McDonald's sex abuse: 'They were waiting for the younger girls to turn 18'

Ex-McDonald's worker: "You need to fix it so it doesn't happen again"

A former McDonald's worker who says a manager failed to protect her when she was being sexualised by customers, has called on the firm to âchange the way they treat younger employeesâ.

Hope, who worked at a branch in the West Midlands in 2023, also told BBC News she saw older colleagues eyeing up 16- and 17-year-old workers, and talking about wanting to sleep with them when they turned 18.

Hope is one of hundreds of workers bringing a discrimination claim against McDonald's. This week, a judge ruled against McDonald's attempt to have their claims struck out, a move that Hope welcomed.

McDonald's said: "Ensuring a safe, respectful and inclusive working environment across our restaurants and offices remains a fundamental priority."

The latest developments come after a BBC investigation in 2023 which heard that workers, some as young as 17, were being groped and harassed.

Last year, McDonald's staff told us they still faced sexual abuse and harassment.

McDonald's workers speak out over sexual abuse claims

McDonald's boss on abuse claims: 'I don't want to talk about the past'

Every McDonald's warned over staff sexual abuse

Hope, who is now 22, says that her branch of McDonalds had a very sexualised, "toxic" environment.

She spoke of a time a manager came up behind her and put his hands on her shoulders. From then on, whenever she saw him coming over, she says she "moved quick" to get away from him.

Another time, she says a manager failed to protect her when she says she was being sexualised and objectified by two customers on a night shift.

Hope says she told the manager what was happening, but he told her to "just suck it up and deal with it".

Her main concern, however, was for the younger teenagers, who she says weren't being looked after.

"You could tell there were other staff members waiting for their 18th birthdays. That sort of like toxic culture where it's like, 'Oh, you know, it's not illegal now.' It was that feeling from quite a few of them," she says.

"And why would you? That is literally a child still."

Hundreds of current and former McDonald's employees are bringing a discrimination claim against the firm.

The fast-food chain had attempted to have the complaints thrown out but this week at the London Central Employment Tribunal, Judge Glennie ruled that 450 cases can go ahead and they are now expected to be heard at the Employment Tribunal in April.

The BBC understands that these relate to indirect discrimination, not sexual harassment.

Hope told us she was not taking part in the legal case for money, saying they didn't know if they were going to get anything.

Instead, she said it was to try and "change the way they treat younger employees".

"It's going to change the way they treat allegations. It's going to shake up the whole internal process and make life easier for the kids who are now having their first jobs. And it's going to make itâ¯â¯a lot safer, I think, as well.â¯â¯And that's something you can't put a price on, the safety of the young these days.''

She also accused McDonald's of trying to "delay the inevitable" by attempting to have the claims thrown out.

"And the judge quite rightly looked at them and said: 'No. We're not going to do that.'"

The claims were presented by law firm Leigh Day, which is acting for the current and former employees.

The claims focus on the vulnerability of young or female workers in the workplace and follow allegations of widespread harassment and discrimination across McDonald's restaurants in the UK.

Leigh Day partner Kiran Daurka told the BBC: "We're really delighted that the judge has found that these claims can go forward to a full hearing. It's really important for access to justice that these cases are heard."

A McDonald's spokesperson said they could not comment on the specifics of ongoing legal proceedings.

In a statement, they said the company had "embedded an extensive set of robust and far-reaching initiatives" as part of its "steadfast commitment" to its staff, adding that this work continues.

"Any form of harassment, discrimination or inappropriate behaviour is unacceptable and has no place in our restaurants or offices. We are confident that with the guidance and partnership of external experts, we and our franchisees have industry leading safeguarding measures in place," the spokesperson added.

#153. Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

It was during November 2020 that somewhere in San Francisco, a woman called Daniela Amodei phoned a friend, asking for advice.

Daniela oversaw safety and policy at OpenAI, one of the most talked-about AI companies in the world.

But Daniela and her brother Dario - who worked there too - had misgivings over safety levels at OpenAI and thought it could be time to walk away.

So she did what many do when we can't make a decision about something, she phoned a friend. The friend's reply was direct and to the point: "You don't need to be on the phone with me. You already know the answer."

The Amodei siblings and a handful of other colleagues quit. Together, they set up a rival â Anthropic.

Six years on and Anthropic, the maker of leading AI model Claude, has predicted revenues of $52bn in 2026 and $135bn in 2027, according to analyst estimates.

It is selling shares to the public for the first time in what's known as an Initial Public Offering or IPO, aiming for a valuation of an estimated $1.4tn (Â£1tn), which would propel it straight into the top 10 of the most valuable US companies.

The sale comes at a crucial time when many are wondering whether, amid increasing concern about the potential of AI, the industry's bubble is showing signs of bursting.

Where once investors were "all in" on a technological revolution to rival the railroads, the car, electricity and the internet, some are now hedging their bets, amid arguments that AI prices will be driven down as the technology becomes more common and concerns that a hefty wedge of the AI investment is tech giants investing in each other.

Shares in companies that supply the raw computing power behind AI, like Coreweave and Nebius, have seen their value cut in half while even the mighty Nvidia has lost its crown as the world's most valuable company with its shares down 30% - a staggering $2tn fall in value.

So could the Anthropic IPO reinflate the bubble or will it be remembered as the last gasp of a boom that is heading for a bust?

At an event last week, Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei told JPMorgan Chase's CEO Jamie Dimon that his guiding principle was to "never let your company be worth a single dollar more than it's worth".

The company is selling roughly $81bn worth of shares to the general public and a further $26bn on to other big tech companies. It is comfortably the single biggest money raising event in US corporate history.

It's an old adage that during a gold rush you should buy companies that sell the picks and shovels as opposed to those staking everything on striking gold. In AI's case that's the chip makers, the data centre builders, the energy providers. But Anthropic is arguably the gold itself and this IPO will see what value people put on it, according to Fiona Cincotta from StoneX trading.

"I think the reason that this IPO is so important is basically it's the first ability for the public investors to access a pure play AI trade," she says, adding that previous offerings have been about "other parts of the ecosystem, which are, the semiconductors, the chips, the, data centres. But this is the first time that we were actually able to access the AI provider."

Daniela Amodei helped set up a rival company to OpenAI

What a company is worth depends almost entirely on predicted future earnings.

It's that calculation that many think presents the sharpest needle to the AI bubble.

Will current and future users of AI be prepared to pay enough to justify the trillions being spent on the chips, data centres, energy and employees, some of whom can command annual salaries of over $100m?

"AI is certainly in a financial bubble. While LLMs [large language models] offer genuine utility and are undoubtedly here to stay, the economics don't add up," said Gary Marcus, Professor Emeritus at New York University and author of Taming Silicon Valley, speaking earlier this year.

"[The] more companies build AI models, the [more the] technology becomes commoditised, eliminating meaningful competitive advantages and driving down prices."

So far, the growth rates in Anthropic revenue are extraordinary.

But these explosive growth rates hide a potential problem. Around a quarter of that revenue came from just a handful of companies, among them Google and Amazon.

It's also concentrated in the US which accounts for two-thirds of its revenue.

There is also the staggering network of circular financing found in the AI world. For example, Anthropic has agreements to buy or lease hundreds of billions of dollars worth of chips and computing power from a handful of companies including Broadcom, Google, Amazon. Many of those firms are also investors in Anthropic and major customers of its products.

It's not just some Wall Street investors questioning whether the AI boom is all it's cracked up to be. Out on Main Street, ordinary Americans have concerns too.

With the US driving so much of global AI growth, a growing public backlash against a technology that threatens to replace millions of jobs and hoover up vast amounts of energy and water became a factor in the recent midterm elections in the US.

Many areas in the US have passed moratoriums on data centre development and many candidates deemed too close to big tech found that association a political liability rather than an asset. Many Democrat candidates publicly opposed data centre development and won.

Added to that, the Pied Piper of AI, chip maker Nvidia's CEO Jensen Huang, resigned last week after admitting to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang and another with a company executive who reported to him.

Dario Amodei is one of Silicon Valley's big beasts

Huang's spectacular fall from grace and Nvidia's 30% fall in value from its peak have left AI investors looking for a new champion for the AI boom that has underpinned the US stock market and arguably much of its entire economy for the last two years.

If Anthropic and its CEO are to be AI's new Pied Piper, he will be playing a different tune to the relentlessly upbeat, leather jacket-clad talisman Huang.

While Huang wanted AI development to continue full throttle, Anthropic was built on misgivings over the levels of safety and responsibility the OpenAI leavers felt were being exercised over the development of society-changing technology.

Amodei has warned that AI capabilities could enable biological weapon development, mass surveillance, authoritarianism and the replacement of millions of white-collar, tax-paying workers.

He has called for a slowdown in its development as its powers are growing at such an exponential rate that it could very soon elude any human oversight.

That said, his paternalistic overtures have been sceptically received by those who point out that the pace of Anthropic's development of new more powerful models has shown no sign of the slowdown he's advocating for the rest of the industry.

But for all the concerns, just as with Elon Musk's SpaceX before it, the hype and the FOMO (fear of missing out) on an undoubtedly profound moment in technological and perhaps human development, means that buying interest is high.

It's reported that orders to buy shares exceed the number on offer by over six times.

With demand outstripping supply by such a margin, a first-day jump is to be expected given the oversubscription, but it doesn't tell you much about long-run return.

Using SpaceX as an example again, shares initially rose nearly 70% from its IPO sale price of $135 to $225. Shares in SpaceX are currently back to $145.

The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful and blow a bit of fresh new air into what some maintain still looks a lot like a bubble.

Eileen Burbidge, founder of tech investors Passion Capital, is one of those who reject the notion that AI is a bubble about to burst.

"Maybe the growth of the bubble has slowed down a little bit, but actually I wouldn't be willing to say that it's about to burst yet," says Burbidge. "I think this IPO could actually mean everyone could be off to the races again."

Fiona Cincotta believes Anthropic's IPO is about far more than just Anthropic. "I think what's going to be really interesting here is I think it can actually be used as a valuation benchmark for the entire AI ecosystem," she says.

"That's why I don't think this is just going to affect the share price of Anthropic. I think this is going to have much wider implications across the whole AI trade."

Two things can be true at the same time. The Anthropic IPO could be wildly successful - FOMO is a powerful emotion in financial markets. But its probable success won't soothe the unease in some quarters that not every company, every investor and every citizen will think of AI as the answer to humanity's problems.

#152. California targets Nvidia in China AI chip probe

California has served an investigative subpoena on the AI chip designer Nvidia in the wake of a scandal that ended in the departure of longtime CEO Jensen Huang.

The state's top law enforcement officer, attorney general Rob Bonta, said he is probing whether Nvidia or Huang's actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information, adding that such actions could violate California law if the company is found to have mislead investors and the public.

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment.

"Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: Companies cannot mislead their investors," Bonta said on Thursday.

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

Nvidia has its global headquarters in Santa Clara, California.

Earlier this month, it emerged that Huang had an extramarital relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang, who was an executive at the Singapore-based data centre company Megaspeed.

Some members of the US Congress have urged the Department of Commerce to investigate whether Megaspeed, a major Nvidia customer, diverted Nvidia chips to China.

Megaspeed has previously said it is aware of reports "containing assertions about our business, supply chain and global operations that are not supported by verifiable evidence".

Nvidia shares have plummeted 22% since the New York Times reported on the affair in early November.

Earlier this week, the company's board chair Harvey Jones said Nvidia had no intention of reinstating Jensen Huang after the longtime Nvidia boss and co-founder issued a statement saying he would be "honoured" to return, as some tech leaders including Elon Musk campaigned on his behalf.

Huang has acknowledged engaging in extramarital affairs â and left the company last week after reports that he had relationships with subordinates.

Nvidia says it won't reinstate Huang as it reports soaring sales

#150. Anthropic becomes one of world's most valuable public companies in stock market debut

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic has become one of the most valuable public companies in the world after its shares began trading on the stock market, taking its valuation to roughly $1.76 trillion (Â£1.34 trillion).

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's Thursday debut on the Nasdaq stock exchange was a crucial test for the industry, given the huge amount of money being poured into advancing the technology.

Anthropic's shares flew past its allocation price of $83 each, showing there was plenty of excitement around AI.

Yet the company, which is behind AI chatbot Claude, will remain under intense scrutiny in the months ahead to answer the key question surrounding AI: is it all worth it?

In just a few hours of trading, Anthropic's stock seemed to show that investors remain uncertain of where exactly AI is heading. The share price rose immediately to $111.53, sending the company's market value to almost $2 trillion, before falling by the end of the day as the shares closed at $97.17.

Some investors said Anthropic's debut seemed to be a success, as shares rose roughly 17% during the day, but the broader tech market did not perform as well.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index closed down nearly 2.3%. Stocks fell in the largest tech firms, including Google's parent company Alphabet, which was down by 2.9%, while Amazon lost almost 3.9%.

The buildout happening around this era of AI is among the most expensive endeavours in the history of technology, with more than $1 trillion being spent in the last year alone by a handful of major tech companies.

The speed at which investment has grown has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble.

"Bubbles don't burst on the day of the largest deal," Richard Whittle, an economist with the University of Salford said. "They burst later, if the positive future becomes suspect."

Anthropic has made one of the fastest trips from start up to IPO in history

While the basics of the technology have been around for decades, the more recent advent of more powerful computers and related components have allowed the processing of more data than ever.

The result has been AI chatbots that simulate human speech and operate tasks like software coding, digital image generation, and searching the internet more quickly.

It represents a new way of using computers that has led to the rapid rise of Anthropic, which started just five years ago and is best known as the maker of the popular AI chatbot Claude.

As the first purely AI company to go public, Anthropic will almost certainly see significant swings in price on the stock market going forward, according to Ben Barringer, who leads technology research at Quilter Cheviot.

The "initial exuberance" will wear off, Barringer said, pointing to what happened with SpaceX earlier this year.

That company, led by Elon Musk, saw shares jump by about 10% on the first day of trading, taking the company to a record-breaking valuation of $2.2 trillion.

Within a month shares had fallen below their debut price, and have mostly fallen further since.

Most tech companies see their shares fall after hitting the stock market, often after reporting quarterly results for the first time.

But many such companies, including Meta, Amazon and Google, have gone on to become the largest and most profitable corporations in the world.

With Anthropic, investors are not only looking at a notable tech company with a major valuation, and a potentially big future in business, but a company that is the first of its kind on the stock market.

"There's no obvious peer to compare it to," Neil Wilson, an investor strategist at Saxo UK, said.

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

Daniela Amodei: Anthropic and the price of AI

So, as individual investors and even researchers and investors at large funds ask themselves if Anthropic is really worth more than $1.7 trillion, or even more, there will be few answers available.

"We'll have a stab at it," Wilson said. "I don't think anyone really knows."

A few things will likely impact the company's success on the stock market in the near-term.

One of those things is the prospect of new laws governing the use of AI tools. In the US, where Anthropic is based, Democrats will be in many more positions of power come 2027, and several politicians have already said they will be looking to ensure AI tools are safe and have some guardrails attached.

Such regulation is currently unlikely at the federal level. US President Donald Trump on Thursday spoke about AI during the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in China, alongside Chinese and Russian counterparts, Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin.

Trump reiterated his position against "any form of robust regulation" around the technology, adding, "we won't allow that to happen, frankly."

The same day, however, a bipartisan group of US lawmakers formed the House AI Safety and Innovation Caucus, which will work specifically on AI regulation and safety considerations.

A significant driver of this kind of scrutiny is Anthropic itself. Company executives, including co-founder and CEO Dario Amodei, have been outspoken in their purported fears that future AI tools will become so capable they will outsmart humans, to the point where extinction is possible.

Anthropic has always put itself forward in public as an AI company that takes the safety of its technology very seriously.

Daniela Amodei, Anthropic's president and sister of Dario Amodei, boasted on Thursday that the company "had an AI safety team before we launched our first product".

Despite the company's claims that the technology it is building at a rapid pace could be dangerous, Anthropic has made one of the quickest trips from start-up to IPO in history.

Anthropic raises $81bn in record-breaking stock market debut

#148. S&P 500 correction: Five forces behind the market sell-off

Esteemed Wharton professor Jeremy Siegel was more animated than usual on Friday afternoon... with good reason.

The normally staid author of "Stocks for the Long Run" spoke to CNBC on Friday as the market careened toward some ugly milestones. The S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average closed in correction territory, off more than 10% from their records. The Nasdaq Composite lost its gain for the year and the more concentrated Nasdaq-100 entered bear market territory on an intraday basis.

"These things are like straws that break the camel's back. You know what I mean? Everyone [is] all, 'I don't care, I don't care cause everything else is good' and when those straws keep on going... Bang," said Siegel.

Just a month-and-a-half ago, the S&P 500 and the Nasdaq Composite were at record highs. But the straws were already adding up by then and a market in denial is just now recognizing them.

Here are the reasons behind the sell-off:

The breaking point came this week in the form of the initial public offering of artificial intelligence leader Anthropic and a pick-up in the selling intensity for one-time AI chip leader Nvidia.

Anthropic upped its offering amount a day before going public despite the already dimming climate around AI-related stocks. The move ensured it would be the biggest IPO ever, but played into the fears that this debut — following SpaceX's monster offering over the summer — was a collective cash-out moment for the AI complex that will leave the investing masses holding the bag.

In other words, it could be the ultimate "sell the news" moment for the AI trade.

On Thursday, Anthropic traded from its $83 IPO price to a high above $115 before instantly rolling over and taking the AI trade with it. Anthropic's sell-off intensified Friday, and when the dust settled Anthropic closed the week at $88.91.

The Nasdaq posted its worst week since the Covid plunge of March 2020, down 10%.

Nvidia fell 14% on Friday with the selling intensifying after a Reuters headline hit the tape that the chipmaker's accounting is the focus of a Securities and Exchange Commission probe.

Nvidia is now down nearly 40% in November alone, a month that saw the AI bull market's leading salesman, Jensen Huang, resign as its CEO because of an affair with an alleged Chinese spy that raised speculation about whether the company's chips were ending up in Beijing's hands despite government restrictions.

To start the week, the Commerce Department tightened restrictions on U.S.-made chips to stop China from accessing them remotely from other countries.

A solid earnings report on Tuesday fell flat as Nvidia's board definitively ruled out any chance Huang would return, disappointing some of the stock's fanatics still holding out hope for his far-fetched return. Nvidia ended the week with a 23% loss.

The S&P 500 traded below its 200-day moving average — what's considered a measure of a security's long-term trend — on Tuesday and made a convincing break of it on Thursday. This turned many on Wall Street— like commodity trading advisors — into automatic sellers on the market. So-called CTAs are individuals or organizations that advise others on trading futures and swaps across a range of assets.

"We know there's a lot of trend riders on these markets," said Siegel. "They have their trend lines. They've ridden it up and as soon as it goes down, they're selling and they're selling and they're selling and they just perpetuate" the decline, he said.

The Iran conflict has caused the price oil to surge and while it's off its highs it still remains above $83 a barrel, while the fees for transiting the Strait of Hormuz and the rest of the region keep persistent pressure on global prices.

A series of numbers earlier in the month showed inflation is still far from the Federal Reserve's 2% target, sparking a run of central bank officials to warn they may have to get more aggressive with interest rates.

After increasing rates in September by a quarter point, the Fed skipped a hike in late October and sparked concern among bond investors it may be falling behind the inflation fight. The 10-year Treasury yield subsequently jumped above 5% and stayed there until a flight of safe haven fixed income buying brought it back below that level on Friday.

Federal Reserve Governor Christopher Waller said this week that the Fed may need to do a half percentage point hike next month to catch up.

"I'm very, very concerned about inflation. It's kind of headed in the wrong direction, and it's spreading out," Waller told CNBC.

"Don't fight the Fed" has long been a maxim on Wall Street and right now expectations are that the central bank will need to raise rates in December and again in January.

And late-breaking on Friday was a report from the Financial Times that the Trump Administration is considering restarting strikes on Iran.

Before the grim market milestones were met on Friday, it had long lost its mojo on fears that the pace of growth driven by AI companies was slowing.

As Anthropic prepared to go public, its third-quarter figures released earlier this month showed revenue growth last quarter was 47%, down from 144% in the second quarter.

AI momentum trades, like the VanEck Semiconductor ETF and the iShares MSCI USA Momentum Factor ETF, all topped out during the summer.

The fears of slowing growth picked up this month after the Democrats won control of Congress and key governorships, fueling speculation that states — or even the whole country — could implement stricter rules on data center construction.

CoreWeave, which builds data centers using high amounts of debt, is down 71% from its high. Caterpillar, which has reinvented itself as an AI play because of data center construction and generators, is down more than 50% from its high.

The view of Siegel and many market observers is that the economy remains in good enough shape that this will just be a run-of-the-mill correction that resets a bull market that had raised people's expectations too high because of its early ferocity.

The S&P 500 returned more than 20% in 2023 and 2024 and then gained 16% last year.

"The average over long periods for the stock market is 8%, 9% a year up," said Siegel. "People began to think that 15 to 20 [percent] was going to be the norm with AI and we have to bring that down to reality."

The only problem with that theory is that the stock market and its wealth effect was one of the major factors underpinning the economy. Stocks are a greater source of household wealth — especially for baby boomers — than they ever have been before.

With the S&P 500 clinging to a 1% gain for the year, this stock market rout could end up tipping off a virtuous cycle that slows the economy, and in turn, leads to bigger equity losses.

#147. A24 is having a great year at the box office. That could give HBO Max a streaming edge

Independent movie studio A24 has had a banner year at the box office, and HBO Max is set up to benefit from its success.

A24 is behind some of this year's buzziest films, including horror hit "Backrooms," dark comedy "The Drama" starring Zendaya and Robert Pattinson, and the Elizabeth Holmes documentary "You Can See Everything," co-directed by Nathan Fielder.

The studio has generated more than $526 million at the domestic box office this year as of Nov. 15, according to data from Rentrak. That's already roughly $290 million, or more than 50%, above its haul for all of 2025.

"They have an identity all their own, and just the mere mention of A24, the recognition that a movie is from A24, gives it instant credibility and provides a must-see factor, no matter what the subject matter," Paul Dergarabedian, head of marketplace trends at Rentrak, told CNBC.

Skydance co-CEO and Chairman David Ellison cited movie releases from A24 as part of the company's "unmatched portfolio" during its most recent earnings call.

A24 films released this year broke records at the box office, with "Backrooms" becoming the studio's biggest domestic opening to date and "You Can See Everything" notching the biggest documentary opening since "Fahrenheit 9/11" in 2004.

That success has already begun to translate to HBO Max's platform.

When "Backrooms" began streaming on HBO Max, it was the most streamed title on the platform for two weeks, according to Nielsen data. The film was also the fourth overall most streamed title the week it premiered, racking up 226 million minutes of viewing time.

After more than a year of competing bids and regulatory hurdles, Skydance now oversees two of the world's largest streaming platforms: HBO Max and Paramount+.

The company plans to eventually merge the streaming services into a single platform. It will take the first step in that effort by offering a value bundle in early 2027.

The combination of the platforms "will drive stronger engagement and higher lifetime value," co-CEO Ynon Kreiz said on the company's earnings call earlier this month.

Paramount Skydance's direct-to-consumer segment, which includes Paramount+ and its free, ad-supported channel, Pluto TV, generated more than $2 billion in revenue during the third quarter, representing more than 35% of total revenue for the period. That segment revenue marked an 11% year-over-year increase, with revenue from Paramount+ up 21%.

The earnings do not reflect the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, since that deal closed after the close of the quarter.

Revenue growth in streaming will be important as it faces the daunting task of managing nearly $80 billion of debt.

The company has said it expects to realize $6 billion in cost savings over the next three years and has put a particular focus on consolidating its operations and streamlining its marketing spend.

#146. Tens of thousands of UK fridges secured from hackers in secret nationwide operation

Sweden was among countries to report a cyber incident in which some supermarket fridge units had been hiked up in temperature

A behind the scenes cyber-security operation has taken place to secure tens of thousands of fridges and freezers at major UK supermarkets, warehouses, restaurants, petrol stations and stadiums from hackers, the BBC has learned.

Recent waves of cyber-attacks in Europe have seen tonnes of food spoiled at hundreds of stores, causing millions of pounds of damage.

France has blamed Iran for one of the waves of attacks that impacted its supermarkets, a claim Tehran denies.

It was thought the UK had largely escaped such hacks as the British refrigeration industry relies on different devices but there was in fact a potential vulnerability the sector has been rushing to fix.

In late October, security researchers discovered a major flaw that, if found by hackers, could have led to even worse damage to the UK.

Since then, researchers, IT teams and the National Cyber Security Centre have been working around the clock to warn dozens of companies.

The BBC has not reported on the large-scale, secretive operation until now to reduce the chances of hackers causing harm if they found the vulnerability.

Tech Life: The everyday tech at risk of hacks

As Europe sees a wave of cyber-attacks on commercial fridges, we investigate how everyday devices are vulnerable to basic attacks using easily-available tools.

The security threat was discovered by staff at refrigeration consultants Omega Solutions - it was then verified and expanded upon by experts at Pen Test Partners who helped raise the alarm.

The National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), the Department for Environment, Food & Rural Affairs (Defra) and Food Standards Agency (FSA) were then involved in the campaign to plug the security holes.

They held industry conference calls and tasked teams to contact firms at risk.

Thousands of devices controlling tens of thousands of refrigeration devices were taken off the open internet either remotely or, in some cases, physically - with staff ordered to remove internet cables and monitor temperatures manually.

BBC news has learnt most major supermarkets have been forced to divert IT teams to address the risks, with a worker at one large chain saying they took hundreds of devices offline every day over the course of a week.

"It did feel like a race against time as we were expecting another wave of these fridge hacks to come," said the person, who did not want to be publicly named.

"From a business perspective it could have been potentially devastating with business interruption and supply chain problems. Our warehouses have millions of pounds worth of food that could have been spoiled if any hackers found out about this method of attack," they added.

There have been three waves of hacks on refrigeration temperature controllers so far this year:

In August, fridges at a small number of US military bases were impacted

On 11 and 12 October hundreds of supermarkets and food processing sites in France were hit when Danfoss temperature control systems were targeted

On 1 and 2 November dozens of supermarkets across seven European countries were hit, with Belgium and Luxembourg the worst affected. A small number of blood bank sites in France were also impacted

The waves of attacks prompted an expert at Omega Solutions to examine the security of the most popular refrigeration controllers in the UK, made by British firm Resource Data Management (RDM).

RDM disputes the critical nature of the vulnerabilities exposed but has been praised for its professional response to address the concerns, the BBC understands.

As well as supermarkets, multiple other areas of UK food and beverage industry were also warned to take action about the risks.

These include convenience stores, petrol stations, restaurants and fast food chains, sports arenas, hotels, food processing plants and cold storage sites.

Each RDM controller can be used to remotely control the temperature of dozens of fridges and freezers

"These popular controllers had a similar weak password problem to the Danfoss and other controllers that were attacked so successfully in previous hacks so we had to get the message out as soon as possible and keep it secret," said Ken Munro of Pen Test Partners.

"There is so much extra attention on these ubiquitous bits of kit now that it was likely a matter of days until criminals or nation state hackers figured it out," he added.

RDM, though, says comparisons with the units attacked in mainland Europe are not fair because hacking their fridges and freezers would have involved getting hold of a piece of hardware.

The ethical hackers say this would have been achievable as the hardware in question can be purchased cheaply online on sites like eBay.

"This was a vulnerability identified in a test environment," said Ged Evans, CEO of RDM.

He said the test "went beyond" what such exercises usually involved, as it "included dismantling our hardware".

"In any event we have acted at pace to address this issue and have already deployed first patch releases and have been in proactive contact with our customers to flag any issues and recommend mitigatory steps," he added.

RDM confirmed that there was no sign of any malicious activity on the controllers and that it continued to work with the NCSC and other authorities to monitor the situation.

What are your thoughts on this topic? Let us know

#144. Mouldy, infested housing for vulnerable people sold as ethical investments abroad

Many of the companies marketing supported housing investments were linked to developer Lee Jones

"This building is hell on Earth," says Phil, describing the black mould and rats at Castleview House, the block of flats in Runcorn, Cheshire, where he lives.

But to an estate agent called BritskÃ© Reality - which translates as British Real Estate - working with investors hundreds of miles away in the Czech Republic, the flat Phil rents through his benefits was an exciting business opportunity.

The estate agent's marketing material said the flat was a government-funded source of income for investors, where tenants were taken care of and the property was in excellent condition.

A Czech investor bought it last year for Â£163,150, more than double the Â£75,000 cost of similar apartments in the block.

The BBC has found more than 40 companies marketing similar properties to individuals in the UK and abroad, as businesses seek to profit from a government system intended to provide housing and support for vulnerable people.

About a dozen of these companies work with a property developer called Lee Jones, whose company sold Phil's flat.

The homes they are selling to investors are advertised as supported accommodation, a type of housing for people with issues including mental health problems or drug abuse issues, as well as people fleeing domestic violence.

The BBC saw mould on ceilings and overflowing rubbish when we visited Castleview House

Providers offering what is known as supported exempt accommodation are allowed to charge the taxpayer more than the usual housing benefit cap, because they have higher running costs. Tenants are entitled to care, support and supervision - although how much support should be provided is not defined.

While there are legitimate providers helping people back into independent living, the BBC has previously reported on how some are exploiting the system by taking the higher housing benefit payments but failing to provide support.

Our latest investigation found companies marketing supported housing properties to people in Hong Kong, Macau, Nigeria, Turkey, the Netherlands, the UK and other countries as a "secure", "government-backed" investment.

We also found adverts made misleading promises about the quality of the accommodation and support, with tenants saying they were receiving little or no care, while we observed examples of poor quality, unsafe accommodation.

Housing charities and experts have told the BBC the sector has been transformed into a profit-generating machine at the expense of some tenants' safety, care, and dignity.

Labour MP Abena Oppong-Asare, who chairs the Housing Select Committee, said it was "outrageous" that supported housing properties were being marketed as investments "particularly where there's public funds involved".

The BBC's findings were "alarming" and showed a sector "open to abuse", she added.

Promotional materials such as this social media post advertise the homes as "Secure, Government-Backed Wealth"

Lee Jones's company The Investment Room converted Castleview House in 2019 from an office block into more than 200 residential flats, including the one where Phil now lives.

Then Jones partnered with Nurture Housing Association. His company granted Nurture a 25-year-lease on some of the flats, to provide housing for people on government benefits. Jones has a close business relationship with Nurture's chief executive Chris Smith, with the pair also acting as joint directors of other housing companies.

Jones then worked with estate agents, which marketed the housing to investors in the UK and overseas using the regular cashflow generated by the 25-year lease with Nurture and the promise of government funding to justify inflated prices.

Lee Jones and Nurture have repeated this with property developments across England, including in Doncaster, Rotherham, Stockton-on-Tees and Bradford. These type of properties are also in Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland but are funded and regulated differently.

Because neither profit-making private companies nor individuals can act as supported housing providers themselves, co-operation between developers, investors and publicly funded providers has become common.

The Czech marketing material found by the BBC said Nurture provides housing for people with mental health issues who receive the "aftercare program pursuant to Section 117 of the Mental Health Act", meaning they have been sectioned and discharged from hospital with a detailed care plan.

But Phil says he has never been sectioned under the Mental Health Act.

He does have severe depression and anxiety, and he is currently out of work. But he says he has never had any support or care, or met anyone working for Nurture during his time at the flat.

"Whoever marketed this property, the way they've worded it is total dishonesty," says Phil. "If they're looking to make as much profit as they can, then they clearly have no plans to fix the issues in the building."

Marketing images appeared to show Castleview House as newly refurbished with bright white interiors

Phil had no idea the flat he rents had been sold to overseas investors in May 2025 until the BBC contacted him. But there were clues: bills addressed to someone called Jan HÃ¡la had been coming through the letterbox for months.

Lee Jones told us that the properties his companies provide are "good quality accommodation" and he has no responsibility for the communal areas of Castleview House since he sold the freehold in 2021. He denied his relationship with Chris Smith amounted to a conflict of interest, but did not respond to questions about his role in marketing the properties.

Jan HÃ¡la, the Czech investor who now owns Phil's flat, and the BritskÃ© Reality estate agency did not respond to requests for comment.

We spoke to several other people living in flats run by Nurture Housing Association in Castleview House who said they were in supported accommodation. They reported receiving little or no help from Nurture but were afraid to speak out publicly for fear of eviction and becoming homeless.

There is also evidence that marketing materials for investors buying flats in Castleview were misleading. Investors were told Nurture was providing "specialist supported housing" - a claim which is repeated on its website.

According to the Regulator of Social Housing, this would mean it was providing a high level of care, equivalent to living in a care home.

But Nurture had not been registered as a specialised supported housing provider, the regulator said, and its claim to be providing this was "shocking".

In a statement, Nurture acknowledged that Castleview House is managed under a "general housing management model" and does not form part of its supported housing portfolio. It said it provided supported housing elsewhere through partners which were registered providers.

It said the Czech advert found by the BBC related to "historic marketing material" produced by third parties.

However, the BBC has seen marketing material from several other agents selling flats in Castleview House to investors, which also claim that Nurture is providing supported housing to the residents on a 25-year lease.

Nurture said it is not directly involved with the companies marketing the properties. But it said: "We will be reviewing communications with relevant agents and reminding them of the importance of ensuring that any information relating to Nurture is accurate, transparent and does not create misunderstanding regarding the accommodation or services provided."

While there are doubts about what kind of housing Nurture has been providing, the BBC also found other flats sold to investors overseas with tenants who were in supported exempt accommodation.

The web of companies involved in providing this housing and cross-border ownership means it can be difficult for vulnerable tenants to get help when there are problems with properties.

"The way that the current system works is there's different levels of involvement by different organisations and companies and no-one is directly accountable," Abena Oppong-Asare, the housing committee chairwoman, told us.

Mo, who has mental health issues, was housed in a studio flat in Cricklewood, north London, after he became homeless. The converted house containing his flat was bought by Gemelli London 2 Limited, a company run by French nationals living in the Czech Republic, for Â£1.83m in January last year.

Mo (right) pictured with a friend who also lived in the same building, says his ceiling collapsed and was not fixed for months

He says his supported housing provider, Windrush Alliance, receives nearly Â£2,000 a month via Barnet Council for the flat. Mo says he receives Â£340 in universal credit for living expenses every month - but he is expected to pay Windrush a monthly Â£120 service charge out of that.

Last year his ceiling collapsed after a leak went unrepaired for more than a year and Mo was temporarily housed in another flat in the property.

Mo says his weekly visits from a Windrush support worker feel like a tick-box exercise and do not help him with the mental health issues which have been keeping him out of work.

Gemelli's director Stephane Boussier told the BBC that when the company bought the property "no complaints from residents were disclosed". It was made aware of issues in August 2025, he said, and since then has "raised them with Windrush in writing, inspected the property and pressed for them to be resolved".

He added that Gemelli London 2 Limited receives about Â£1,365 per flat per month from Windrush and does not receive any of the extra housing benefit that would be used for vulnerable tenants.

Windrush said it was aware of issues at Mo's property and was "working with the building owner to put them right". According to Gemelli, the ceiling was repaired after the BBC contacted the two companies.

Windrush has previously been criticised by the Regulator for Social Housing for a "failure to meet the standards" of the regulator, including failing to ensure "tenants are safe in their homes".

It acknowledged that it "fell short in the past". It wrote off Â£1.8m in "funds to former directors and companies under their control", saying the company's financial position had been "compromised" by their actions. The company now has a new board in place.

Windrush told us its "priority is residents, not investors" and the rates it charges "are agreed with the relevant local authorities" and "any surplus is reinvested in our homes and services".

In Bradford, the BBC visited City Exchange and Colonnade House, two former office blocks which Lee Jones has converted into housing - with Nurture Housing Association managing many of the flats.

Many of the properties there were advertised as supported housing investments. Residents showed us cockroach infestations, faulty fire alarms, broken lifts, leaks through the ceiling and black mould.

Nurture Housing said it was only responsible for the individual homes that it manages and does not manage communal areas or "wider building infrastructure".

"Nurture Housing and its managing agents regularly raise these concerns with the block manager," the company said.

The freeholder for Phil's block, Castleview House, and City Exchange, blamed "defects" by the developer Lee Jones for the problems with the buildings and said it was taking legal action. Colonnade House has had several building management companies in recent years and the latest said it was addressing the problems.

None of these companies or individuals are breaking the law, but critics say they are exploiting a lack of regulation in the sector.

Three years after the Supported Housing Act was passed in England, which would put in place a licensing regime for supported housing providers and give local councils more powers to shut down providers that failed to meet the standards, it still has not been implemented.

"It's taken too long," said Jasmine Basran, head of policy and campaigns at housing charity Crisis. "In the meantime, this an area where we've seen the worst kind of exploitation."

Additional reporting by Lorna Acquah and Abi Smitton
