#143. Airbnb's divisive new house rules come into force

New rules on what Airbnb hosts can reasonably ask guests to do have come into force.

Hosts should now not normally ask guests to wash up, for example, but can appeal to Airbnb if they have a compelling reason.

Some guests have found lists of house rules laborious to complete, with one telling the BBC "it was not relaxing at all".

But some hosts have said that the costs of paying for extra cleaning may have to be passed on to customers.

The new Airbnb rules, announced last month, allow hosts to choose from seven standard house rules, which include determining how many people can stay overnight, and whether or not pets or smoking are allowed.

Hosts can then set additional rules, but guests shouldn't be asked to do the washing up, remove sheets from beds, take rubbish out or avoid using utilities.

Airbnb has begun reviewing listings and will be sending emails to hosts, external "who need to take action" it said.

Hosts can appeal to Airbnb to argue an additional house rule is necessary, and ask Airbnb to approve it.

If the house rule is not approved and the host keeps it anyway, Airbnb will flag this to guests on the host's listing page with a message such as "includes cleaning tasks".

Airbnb said chores can make customers hesitate about booking through the platform, arguing that making house rules "clearer" would increase the number of clients.

But some hosts have said they may have to pass the extra cleaning costs on to guests.

Lisa Clewes, who runs a Welsh holiday let, told the BBC she was "horrified" at the state her guests left the house after a dinner party.

There were broken glasses, drinks cans on the floor, pans full of food on the table and fish guts left in the garden.

Clewes said it typically takes cleaners four hours to turn around her three-bedroom property.

She said guests leaving more mess could mean hiring extra cleaners and increasing costs â expenses that may ultimately be passed on to her customers.

Airbnb host Lisa Clewes said her Welsh holiday let was left 'a complete mess' by a group of guests

Edinburgh-based holiday home owner Fiona Venter told the BBC that Airbnb's new rules were "nonsense" and would not accept bookings if guests refuse to take their rubbish away.

Talking about a time when she was worried she might get a mice infestation after six bags of rubbish were left in her cottage, she said: "I would rather have my house still standing."

But Fiona said increased cleaning costs due to the rule change would be unfair to those who do clean up after themselves: "Why should they pay extra for the guests that choose to abuse a cottage?"

Airbnb hosts can no longer make guests strip beds and wash dishes under new rules

No more washing up: How Airbnb's new house rules have divided hosts and guests

The changes have come as a relief to guests like Jessica Newhook, who says she has been faced with lengthy checkout lists, often on top of hefty cleaning fees.

She booked an Airbnb in Florida for a family reunion of 11 guests and says they had to "leave the property move-in ready for the cleaners", despite paying a $250 (Â£187) cleaning fee.

"We had rules about everything and it was not relaxing at all," she said.

The new rules have added to growing frustration among owners following increases in Airbnb's commission rates last year.

Many are investing in their own websites and social media to attract direct bookings and avoid platform fees.

Not all hosts are concerned about the changes, however.

Matt Porsz, who hosts and manages other Airbnb properties full-time, told the BBC previously the review system encourages both hosts and guests to behave reasonably.

#142. Fish and eggs al desko? The dos and don'ts of lunch at work

There are two types of office workers: those who eat lunch al-desko, and those who have to endure the smell of everyone else's.

Few things are more likely to test workplace relationships than the person sitting next to you proudly unveiling a container of reheated fish.

From tuna sandwiches and boiled eggs to crumbly and sticky treats that leave a mess on the keyboard, what is considered OK to eat in the office can be surprisingly divisive.

"We all know not to bring in a microwave fish pie, that is the law," says civil servant Sarah.

She admits she has, in fact, committed the offence herself in a previous workplace and jokes "that's the reason I left".

Casey, a content creator for an energy company, says he used to eat tuna at his desk but has since "reformed".

He says you have to "suss out the smelliness" and then decide if it's acceptable to eat at the desk.

Casey and Manny often eat at their desk and their favourite pretzel snack is causing some problems given how sticky and crumbly it is

But fish isn't the only food causing office friction and Casey says his regular snack of cinnamon pretzels has been causing "quite a bit of havoc".

"The sugar's going everywhere and it's getting in my laptop keys," he admits. No-one in the office has commented on it yet but he says he's hiding the crumbs by "shuffling them off the desk".

Boiled eggs also split opinion. Laura, who works in finance, says she's been teased by colleagues for eating them.

"In my defence, it was high protein, it was cost-effective and healthy â I swear by a good boiled egg."

She thinks that no food is off limits at the desk. "Coming from an Asian background where you'd eat fish for breakfast I am very open to it being eaten."

Ashlea Fisher says it's important all of her employees are mindful about the food they are eating at their desk

Ashlea Fisher, who runs her own recruitment company, has tackled the issue of smelly food in the office head on when she asked a member of staff to stop bringing in fish after "the smell was so bad that a pregnant colleague was feeling physically sick".

"I'm a big believer in being able to have an honest conversation with your team," she says, and suggests that other people in her position should approach a conversation lightly and be conscious of not embarrassing colleagues.

"Nobody should feel judged for what they eat, and we should respect different cultures and dietary requirements, but being mindful of the people around you is simply good workplace etiquette."

While what we eat in the office at lunch can cause tension, there's also the question of whether we're even taking the time to enjoy our lunch in the first place.

In a world of back-to-back meetings and overflowing inboxes, it's easy to skip our break and shovel down a sandwich with one hand while replying to emails with the other.

A 2025 report by Just Eat found that the youngest workers, Gen Z, were the most likely to take a full break, with 56% doing so.

However, the next generation up, millennials were more likely than any other to eat lunch at their desk.

"While it can be convenient to eat at your desk, it's not best practice to remain glued to the screen all day," says Alan Price, an employment law and HR expert, and CEO of BrightHR.

"Taking a break isn't just an employment right, there are proven benefits for both physical and mental health."

Finance worker Laura takes a lunch and dinner break when she's working long hours

Laura, whose working day can occasionally finish at 2am, says stepping away from her desk is a non-negotiable and she makes a point of taking an hour for lunch, describing it as "essential for maintaining my sanity".

"I take my lunch break as much as possible, for the sake of my eyes and mental wellbeing," she says.

But others are less precious about it. Content creator Manny says he has lunch at his desk "most of the time".

His work is task-based and his company operates a policy of "do the work when you want to do it".

"Usually you just take 20 minutes to eat something, but on another day you might take an hour. You just kind of feel it out," he says.

Do you have comments or questions about this story?

#140. Tinned food and long-life milk boost retail sales after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Retail sales rose in October, driven by people buying long-life food such as tinned goods and UHT milk, according to official figures.

Sales volumes were 0.7% higher as people responded to the government's emergency preparedness "Be Ready" campaign, the Office for National Statistics (ONS) said.

The campaign encouraged people to stock up in case of disruption to essential services by severe weather or cyberattacks.

Separate research released last week suggested that while households were stockpiling essentials, they were not panic buying.

The government campaign encouraged people to keep three days' worth of food and water in their homes.

Wet wipes, a torch and cash were also recommended.

Data from retail research firm Worldpanel by Numerator indicated last week that people were stocking up on items including tinned meat, bottled water and hand sanitiser.

The latest official sales figures, external from the ONS add to the evidence that the public has begun stockpiling.

"Supermarkets have told us that sales of long-life food items such as tinned food and UHT milk were boosted by the government's 'Be Ready' campaign," ONS chief economist Grant Fitzner said.

"Meanwhile fuel sales have been recovering from earlier low levels, despite pump prices rising again."

The rise in retail sales last month beat some economists' expectations - they had predicted a slight fall - and the increase came after a 0.6% decline in September.

As well as sales being boosted by people buying essentials, visits to art galleries and clothing purchases were also higher in October.

The boost in sales comes at the beginning of the festive period, which is normally the busiest time of the year for retailers.

Jacqui Baker, partner and head of retail at auditing firm RSM UK, said that "retailers will want to see out the year with some Christmas cheer before consumers batten down the hatches in the New Year as energy costs skyrocket and inflation ramps up further".

Typical energy bills are forecast to go up by Â£443 per year from January, pushing up the pace of price rises.

Sales of tinned food and bottled water jump after 'Be Ready' campaign launch

Why should I stockpile and what will I need?

Separate economic data released on Friday showed the government borrowed slightly less than economists had expected in October.

Borrowing - the difference between public sector spending and income - was Â£17bn last month, the ONS said., external

Although that was Â£0.8bn higher than October last year, it was below analysts' forecasts of about Â£18bn.

Interest repayments were Â£11.2bn - the highest October debt interest figure on record - and Â£2.8bn more than a year ago.

These repayments have been pushed up by higher inflation, as payments on index-linked government bonds are linked to the inflation rate.

Inflation rose to 3.6% in October, mainly because of higher petrol and diesel prices. The rate has been rising in recent months as the Iran war has driven up the cost of energy and fuel.

#139. Trump says there could be a 'positive spin' from the latest FBI database hack

President Donald Trump suggested Saturday that there could be a bright spot in the latest incursion into the FBI computer database.

The president confirmed in an interview with Fox News that a system has been breached that houses information used by law enforcement at all levels to share criminal justice information.

The hackers accessed the National Criminal Information Center (NCIC), a database that includes FBI fingerprint records, wanted persons, violent felons, missing persons and related information, two people familiar with the matter told MS NOW. The database is restricted to law enforcement use but not classified and it is unclear what data was extracted.

CNN had first reported on the suspected hack, citing sources familiar with the issue.

In a wide-ranging interview on Fox, Trump said there could be upside from the news.

"Maybe we'll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place," he said. "I always like to put things on a positive spin."

This would be the second recent intrusion into the FBI system; the previous one came in September, when a group known as "ShinyHunters" broke into an FBI jobs website. The result compromised data on current and former FBI employees and shut down part of the agency's operations until the issue was resolved.

Trump said it's possible there could be information gleaned from the latest hack.

"I wonder who's doing it, because wouldn't it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?" he said. "People should hear about it, because it actually put our country in danger, and so maybe we'll learn things that we wouldn't have learned otherwise."

The FBI did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

#137. GTA 6 sales hit $3.8bn in a single day - the biggest entertainment launch in history

Grand Theft Auto 6 (GTA 6) saw record retail sales in its first full-day of release, hitting $3.8bn (Â£2.9bn), making it the biggest entertainment launch in history.

Take-Two, the parent company of Rockstar Games, said on Friday the figures were based on estimates through end of day on Thursday and marked a record for "entertainment across any medium". It pre-sold 32.5m copies of the game.

To compare, Spider-Man: Brand New Day, this year's highest-grossing film so far, made $2.5bn (Â£1.8bn) over its entire run in cinemas.

Internet infrastructure firm Cloudflare said earlier that PS5 downloads in the UK peaked at 8.6 times normal levels around its launch.

New Zealand, where GTA 6 was first available, saw a 14.8-fold spike four hours before, suggesting some players switched regions for early access.

In its initial findings since the game's launch, Cloudflare also recorded a huge surge in traffic to Rockstar's online services, peaking at 11.2 times normal levels in Australia and 9.3 times on the US East Coast.

In Europe, UK interest was particularly strong, reaching seven times normal levels compared with 5.3 times in France and Germany.

The record smashes that of its predecessor - from 13 years prior - Grand Theft Auto V. The game previously held the best opening week sales in video-game history, earning $1.15bn (Â£1.2bn) in just five days.

The game is the latest instalment in Rockstar's nearly three decade old crime simulator franchise, which has become one of the most popular games in history.

At its core, the series is a satirical take on the American dream, putting players in the shoes of career criminals in Leonida, the game's Florida-inspired state.

The latest release follows new characters, a pair of small-time criminals Lucia and her partner Jason.

GTA 6 is estimated to have cost more than $1bn to develop, which would make it the most expensive video game in history.

BBC News Mundoâs Rafael Rojas visits the real-life Miami locations that inspired GTA 6's Vice City.

Research firm Sensor Tower said GTA 6 will be so popular that it will reduce the time gamers spend on other single-player titles by 10%.

The firm said its estimates are based on email receipts data and other statistical sources that track millions of people daily. They also track public profiles across major gaming platforms like PlayStation and Xbox, where GTA 6 is available.

Around three quarters of the game's sales have been on PlayStation 5 and the balance on Xbox, Sensor Tower said.

Niko Partners, a gaming market research firm, said it expects GTA 6 to make more than $3bn in sales worldwide this year.

That figure would rank GTA 6 among the highest-grossing entertainment releases ever, but it would still have some way to go to catch 1939's Gone with the Wind, which has made an estimated $4.5bn (Â£3.44bn), adjusted for inflation.

Rockstar is expected to make even more money from GTA 6 over the coming years.

The New York-based studio has yet to announce when the game will be released for PCs or given a date for its multiplayer expansion, which will allow players to interact and go on missions with others over the internet.

If 2013's GTA 5 is any guide, multiplayer will add dozens more hours of gameplay and bring in even more revenue for Rockstar as players spend real money on in-game property, cars and other perks.

GTA 6 launched around the world on 19 November

Anticipation for the blockbuster title has grown through repeated delays, following a 13-year wait.

GTA 6 has been hailed by some reviewers as one of the most impressive gaming experiences ever. Entertainment media site IGN described it as an "uncompromisingly engineered" masterpiece.

The release of GTA 6 at midnight on Thursday across various time zones saw many gamers stay up through the night, while some took the following day off to play.

The launch broke Virgin Media O2's evening traffic record as people rushed to install GTA 6's huge 212GB file on consoles.

The game's graphic content has resulted in censorship in Japan and, like earlier GTA releases, is not approved for sale in mainland China.

Police departments have also reminded Americans that street racing, auto theft and gun violence are still crimes in real life, as excitement reached a fever pitch in the US.

#134. Trump threatens ban on US firms building AI data centres overseas

Trump says he won't allow American companies to build data centres "outside" the US

Donald Trump has said he will ban US companies from building AI data centres overseas, threatening a "tremendous tax" for tech firms who do so.

"We're going to keep it all in this country," the US president said, while providing little detail on the potential policy he said others in his cabinet were hearing about for the first time.

Data centres were a central issue in the midterm elections earlier in November, with many Democratic candidates promising tougher regulation on their construction.

Tech giants including Meta and Google have entered agreements to build data centres overseas. It remains unclear how such a policy would be enforced if it was brought in.

"We're not going to allow our companies to build data centres outside of our country," Trump told reporters in a press briefing following his return from his trip to Asia.

"Because China will take all of them, and we're not allowing that to happen."

But whether that idea will become policy remains unclear.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story, but I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now, they're here," he said.

Trump floated the policy after saying the US was leading on data centre construction, and he would not allow China to beat the US when it came to AI - which he calls super intelligence, or SI.

It also follows an announcement earlier this week that would restrict the export of advanced computer chips and who can access the computing power provided by them.

Washington on Monday announced firms would require a licence to buy advanced chips, so US officials can monitor whether they are being used to serve Chinese companies or they are being smuggled into China.

The move will impact South East Asian countries that have invested heavily in data centres, as Chinese companies seek to rent computing power from overseas.

Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Data centres are crucial to the AI boom. The vast warehouse-style buildings house the advanced computer chips and servers that are needed to run AI programmes such as Claude and ChatGPT.

As of April there were about 3,000 data centres across the US, with another 1,500 in various stages of approval and construction, according to the Pew Research Centre.

Many of them are built in rural areas, and concerns about issues including noise pollution and energy consumption helped drive Democratic candidates promising tougher rules on data centre construction to wins on 3 November.

"You ought to take advantage of the data centres. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but take advantage don't shut them off," Trump said.

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy and they have everything. They're tremendous assets."

Large tech firms - often called AI hyperscalers due to the size of their investments in AI - have also been branching out, and Canada has made moves to step in to offer itself as an alternative location for these sites, with less local pushback against projects so far.

Meta has already broken ground on a data centre, external in the province of Alberta, and Anthropic has signed a long-term agreement to lease a proposed data centre in Nova Scotia.

The BBC has reached out to Meta for comment on Trump's remarks.

In September Google announced a â¬13bn ($15bn: Â£11bn) investment in AI infrastructure in Finland, which included funding for three data centres.

Last month, building work for two of those centres was halted due to concerns over the clearance of more than 300 hectares of forest.

Trump called out the build in Finland specifically, saying his administration was "not allowing that to happen".

#131. Saudi Arabia, Bahrain report new attacks as Iran says it remains open to a deal with U.S.

Saudi Arabia and Bahrain reported attacks by Iran-linked forces over the weekend, as Iran's foreign minister said his government remains open to a deal to end the war with the U.S.

"Intercepted and destroyed a drone launched by the Houthi terrorist militia toward the Riyadh area," the Saudi-led Coalition to Support Legitimacy in Yemen, which has been leading the fight against the Iran-backed group, said Sunday in a post on X.

The coalition said late Saturday that its air defenses "intercepted and destroyed a ballistic missile" launched by the Yemen-based Houthis toward Saudi Arabia's western region, which includes some of the country's sensitive energy refining and export infrastructure.

Neighboring Bahrain also said it came under Iranian fire.

"As a result of the heinous Iranian aggression, a civilian industrial facility was targeted, causing a fire at the site," Bahrain's Ministry of Interior said on X. The ministry later said two people, a Bahraini citizen and a Pakistani resident, were injured in the attack.

Bahrain is home to the U.S. Navy's Fifth Fleet.

Iranian state media reported late Saturday that the country's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps fired missiles and drones at U.S. military facilities in Bahrain.

A separate Iranian media report claimed the IRGC's air defenses intercepted and destroyed a U.S. MQ-9 drone.

U.S. Central Command did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on either Iranian report.

Meanwhile, Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said his government remains open to a negotiated settlement to the war. An agreement aimed at developing a permanent peace deal signed in June between President Donald Trump and his Iranian counterpart Masoud Pezeshkian — the so-called Islamabad Memorandum of Understanding — later broke down.

"The Islamabad Memorandum of Understanding is no longer on the table and has no implementation relevance," Araghchi told the Iran newspaper.

"However, the commitments that the U.S. had accepted in that Memorandum of Understanding and the principle that the implementation of those commitments can pave the way for reaching a final agreement are still accepted by both sides," he said.

The war, which began with U.S. and Israeli airstrikes on Iranian targets on Feb. 28, has resulted in major disruptions to energy shipments through the Strait of Hormuz between Iran and Oman.

The United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations reported that two tankers near Oman were struck by unidentified projectiles on Saturday.

Iran has imposed curbs on vessels moving through the strait, a major sticking point in negotiations with the U.S.

Oil prices have fallen for three straight weeks, however, as traders weighed strong Middle East flows and rising U.S. inventories.

International benchmark Brent crude fell 3.4% for the week, while U.S. crude tumbled 5.2%.

#130. UK to explore even closer ties with EU after agreeing deal, says Burnham

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen, Prime Minister Andy Burnham and European Council President Antonio Costa join hands at the end of a press conference during the EU-UK summit in Brussels

Andy Burnham said the UK will discuss possible options for even closer ties with the EU, after agreeing a deal covering British food exports, emissions trading and a youth experience scheme.

The prime minister said it was a "good day" for Britain and Europe, adding the Treasury believes the deal could boost the UK economy by up to Â£17bn a year.

European Council President AntÃ³nio Costa said the EU remains "open to have the closest possible relationship" with the UK.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said the deal "isn't fair" - and Reform's Nigel Farage called it a "betrayal of the British people".

Legislation to implement the deal is expected to be introduced in the UK Parliament next week.

Burnham arrived in Brussels to finalise the latest stage of the government's post-Brexit "reset" of relations with the EU, which originally began under former prime minister Sir Keir Starmer.

The agreements announced on Friday included scrapping most of the checks on British food exports, in return for the UK following some EU regulations, from 2028.

The UK government believes the changes will cut costs for businesses and could bring prices down for shoppers, while critics argue the change will make the UK a "rule taker" from Brussels rather than a rule maker.

Watch: What the new UK-EU deal could mean for European relations

The new youth experience scheme will allow up to 100,000 18 to 31 year olds from the EU to travel, work and study in the UK for up to three years. The same number of young Britons will be allowed into Europe.

The two sides want to ensure that opportunities under the annual scheme will be available to young people by no later than January 2030.

A further six months is available to discuss a deal on tuition fees, which could prove to be a potential stumbling block.

There have been calls from the European side for fees to be reduced for EU students at British universities. The joint statement says any agreement would also be for UK students in the EU.

Burnham gets his first taste of EU dealmaking and seems hungry for more

What is the EU-UK youth experience scheme and when will it start?

Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said she is "confident" a solution will be found, with Burnham adding: "Me too."

On immigration, the UK and EU have committed to working closely to tackle migrant smuggling and human trafficking. They also expect their law enforcement agencies to share more data.

Burnham said "significant progress" has been made to help "disrupt the gangs who are making those dangerous crossings across the English Channel".

The UK-EU statement also said talks on linking the EU and UK emissions trading systems have concluded and they aim to apply the agreement from 2028.

Watch: Will the UK-EU summit deal boost the economy?

Burnham argued that polling suggests the UK public "want closer ties" with the EU and the deal "delivers that".

He added: "Beyond that we do need to consider what is the right long-term relationship. Incremental steps only take us so far."

The prime minister said his government will publish a 10-year plan next month, adding it will set out its ambitions for reindustrialisation, growth in "all parts of Britain" and opportunities for young people.

Burnham said these ambitions are "connected to our long-term relationship with our nearest partner".

He said over the coming months before the next UK-EU summit both sides will "seek to conduct an informal brainstorming process" to look at what those options are.

Burnham added: "What we are announcing today is probably the limit of what we can achieve in this Parliament, given what needs to be done to implement it.

"But we want to inform the Parliament after this one, so that decisions can be taken about that long-term relationship."

Labour's 2024 general election manifesto pledged there would be "no return to the single market, the customs union, or freedom of movement", and Burnham has committed to this.

The prime minister said the "British people will be the boss of what happens next", with the next election needing to be held by August 2029.

Von der Leyen, who said the summit was a "turning point" for the EU-UK relationship, said "we are open for brainstorming".

Badenoch said the deal was not fair because UK farmers would now have to follow rules made by Brussels and more young people will come to the UK to "compete with our young people who are struggling to get jobs".

Badenoch was also asked about the Treasury's analysis, which Burnham said suggested the deal "will add up to 0.5%" to UK gross domestic product and he described it as a "Â£17bn boost to the British economy".

She claimed Burnham has "plucked that figure out of the air" and "the only modelling I've seen that even talks about that figure says it's actually 0.0%".

Farage said Friday was a "very good day for the EU", adding: "A good day to try and humiliate the one member state that actually had the courage to say, 'We don't need to be part of you, we can govern our own country'."

In a social media post, Farage, who was a leading figure in the 2016 campaign to get Britain out of the EU, added: "Like every prime minister before him, Burnham is a total fraud. Only Reform will stop the endless betrayal of the British people."

Liberal Democrat leader Sir Ed Davey said there needed to be more than "tweaks" to the UK's relationship to the EU.

"A youth mobility scheme is a big step forward, but it's nowhere near enough. We need a whole new deal that actually works for Britain, and fast."

#127. MP criticises Guernsey and Jersey company ownership register plans

MP Sir Andrew Mitchell has campaigned for publicly accessible registers of who owns companies in British Overseas Territories and Crown Dependencies

Plans to improve transparency over company ownership in Guernsey and Jersey have come under criticism from a Westminster MP, who says they "do not go nearly far enough".

The comments come after Guernsey, external and Jersey, external set out plans to allow approved applicants access to information about who owns or controls companies when investigating suspected financial crime.

Sir Andrew Mitchell, who has campaigned for greater scrutiny of company ownership, said there were "immediate indications" the proposals would fall short.

He said limits on access to the information could mean "national security threats may remain entirely invisible to investigators".

The plans follow a joint commitment made by Guernsey, Jersey and the Isle of Man in December 2023 to increase transparency around company ownership and help tackle money laundering, terrorist financing and other financial crime.

Currently, information about who ultimately owns or controls companies is generally only available to law enforcement agencies, regulators and certain regulated businesses carrying out anti-money laundering checks.

The new proposals would allow journalists, academics and other applicants with a legitimate interest to apply for access.

Sir Andrew said investigators could face a "catch-22" because they may need access to the register to prove wrongdoing in the first place.

He also said he understood applicants would have to show they had been unable to find the information elsewhere, arguing: "This entirely defeats the object of a register."

Jersey's consultation response says applicants would be expected to show that "all reasonable alternative sources have been exhausted" before access was granted.

Guernsey's published response does not explicitly set out the same requirement.

He said: "Access should be open to those from other jurisdictions, for example those in the global south from whom much of the world's dirty money was stolen in the first place."

Sir Andrew also questioned whether users would have to pay to access the information, saying: "This information should be free at the point of access."

He said the proposals should eventually lead to fully public registers.

Both the governments of Guernsey and Jersey rejected Sir Andrew's criticism and said they continue to meet international standards in tackling financial crime.

A Government of Jersey spokesperson said the concerns raised by him "do not accurately reflect what is in Jersey's consultation response paper".

They said the policy was intended to help Jersey meet its international commitments while maintaining "a fair, proportionate, and effective transparency framework".

They added: "Jersey continues to meet international standards for transparency and countering money laundering, terrorist financing and proliferation financing."

Deputy Andrew Niles, from Guernsey's Policy & Resources Committee, rejected Sir Andrew's criticism and pointed to a positive 2025 assessment of the island's efforts to tackle financial crime, external.

He said: "Guernsey does not accept the suggestion that our proposals fall short of the commitments we've made on beneficial ownership transparency or that the island does not meet international standards on beneficial ownership transparency."

"The proposal is based on a tried and tested model adopted in the EU," he added.

#124. Tech stock sell-off drags markets down as Anthropic shares slide

Watch: How did Anthropic's stock market debut unfold?

Anthropic's shares have dropped back towards their initial price after a soaring first day on the stock market, as a wider tech sell-off dragged markets down.

The sell-off comes as concerns remain over the speed and size of artificial intelligence investment, but also as some large companies including Nvidia and SpaceX faced specific issues that worried investors.

Those pressures combined to weigh on US markets through Friday, with all key US indexes closing sharply down on the day, furthering earlier falls over the week.

Both the S&P 500 and the Dow Jones Industrial Average were pushed into correction territory, a point where they are trading at 10% or more down from a recent high.

The S&P 500 closed down 3.7%, the Dow Jones Industrial Average was down 1.9% and the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite Index plunged 5.3%. However, all three indexes were up on where they were a year ago.

The speed at which AI investment has been growing - with more than $1 trillion spent in the last year alone by a small handful of major tech companies - has led to ongoing concerns of a financial bubble. Questions are being raised over whether those investments will pay off, and when.

Investors were also spooked after the Democrats' resounding win in the US midterms in early November, which sparked fears of a reduction in AI development.

Despite a positive first day of trading, Anthropic's shares fell on Friday, dropping at one point by more than 12% to about $85, close to its initial public offering (IPO) price of $83. It closed down 8.5%, reversing half of the gains made on Thursday.

The artificial intelligence (AI) company's introduction to the Nasdaq stock exchange on Thursday was a crucial test for the industry and was largely seen as a success, with trading starting at $111.50 before easing to end the day up 17% at $97.17.

Analysts said volatility was to be expected in the first days or even weeks of Anthropic's trading, as the company was the first frontier AI firm to go public so had no direct peers.

Some have also noted the similarities with Elon Musk's SpaceX, which at the time of its debut in June was the largest IPO on record. Shares in the rocket and AI company experienced large swings in its first few weeks of trading.

Ed Moya, a strategist at market intelligence firm AlphaSense, said Anthropic's blockbuster IPO did not appear to have restored confidence in the broader AI market.

While AI demand was still very strong, Moya told the BBC that investors were trying to answer the question of how much were they willing to pay for that growth.

"As we've gone through the past past few weeks, it seems that there's been more risks that have surfaced," he said.

"Right now you're starting to see Wall Street is trying to shift from rewarding growth at any price, to demanding stronger cash flow and a clearer path to profitability."

Shares in some of the largest US tech companies also fell on Friday as the market sell-off continued.

Nvidia shares had plummeted by 13.7% by the close of trade, following Reuters reports - unconfirmed by the BBC - the company was being investigated by the US Securities and Exchange Commission over its accounting practices.

Musk's firms Tesla and SpaceX both experienced falls of more than 7.6%. SpaceX shares were hit after it announced a bond sale to finance what the company said was the largest purchase of Nvidia chips on record.

Meta closed down more than 5% and Amazon was down more than 4%, while Apple and Alphabet experienced smaller falls.

Europe's main stock markets - which contain fewer tech stocks - also took a hit.

Germany's Dax index closed down 2.6%, France's CAC 40 was down 1.9% and the UK's FTSE 100 ended trading 1% lower.

Earlier on Friday, tech-heavy Asian markets saw steep falls.

South Korea's Kospi and Japan's Nikkei 225 indexes closed down about 5%, with shares in memory chip makers Sk Hynix, Kioxia and Tokyo Electron, and other tech firms such as Samsung Electronics, seeing losses.

Taiwan's stock market dropped about 6% on Friday, taking the weekly fall to more than 17% and what was reported to be its biggest weekly fall since 1990.

Simon Jack: Can Anthropic's record-breaking IPO stop the AI bubble bursting?

#121. Should AI be judging our credit worthiness?

Sophie Hope's credit score was hit by identity fraud

Getting approval for a mortgage, a car loan or even a mobile phone contract can rest on a credit score.

Sophie Hope was on the sharp end of just such a decision.

"When I first checked my credit score, it was excellent. But then my mortgage application was suddenly denied by the lender because of my credit report. It was a massive shock as I'd never had bad credit. When I looked into it, my score had gone to 'very poor'," she says.

It turns out, the Brighton-based nail technician and content creator, had been the victim of identity fraud.

"Someone had opened accounts in my name across various catalogue sites and ordered multiple items, including a PlayStation 4 and Nike socks," she says.

"Without me knowing, the debt was transferred to a secondary finance company. It spent three to four years accumulating in the background. It was only when I went to apply for a mortgage that I discovered how severely it had dragged down my credit score," she says.

Credit scores are "backward looking" says Funmi Olufunwa

Hope's experience shows how crucial a credit score can be, but, as in her case, it does not tell the full story.

"As a nation, we are overly obsessed with credit scores," says Funmi Olufunwa, a consumer finance lawyer and founder of financial education business, Hoops Finance.

Three firms - Experian, Equifax and TransUnion - produce credit scores in the UK, and they all work from roughly the same data.

"A credit score is backward looking. It's looking at what has happened in the past, which doesn't necessarily predict the future," says Olufunwa.

"But when somebody's looking to lend you money, they're looking at what actually is affordable," she says.

Traditional credit scoring systems also disadvantage "thin-file applicants" - young people and recent immigrants to the UK who won't have built up a credit history.

But with more financial data available than ever before - and a growing appetite for AI to make sense of it - could artificial intelligence help lenders work out who is safe to lend to?

In the US lending decisions will often hinge on a credit score from a company called FICO, which uses data from the big three credit assessment firms.

But AI-powered lending firms, Upstart and Zest AI have both built businesses which offer lenders an extra option when it comes to assessing a client's credit worthiness.

"Thanks to our machine-learning models that look at more than 2,500 variables, we believe we can make significantly more loans at low prices to borrowers from every demographic group," Upstart said in statement.

Mortgage expert and founder and CEO of The Mortgage Mum, Sarah Tucker says: "AI has the potential to help lenders analyse much broader sets of information, identify patterns more quickly and build a more individual picture of someone's affordability and risk."

If technology allows lenders to understand so-called thin-file applicants better, rather than simply applying increasingly rigid rules, Tucker believes there is "a real opportunity to improve financial inclusion".

But like with many other applications of AI into everyday life, there are concerns.

"Consumers need to understand what information is being used to make decisions about them, and they need to be able to challenge something when it is wrong," Tucker explains.

Credit scores have been a contentious topic for decades for this very reason, says Sandra Wachter, professor of Technology and Regulation at the University of Oxford.

"We are not assessing if past decisions were right," Wachter argues, adding, AI does not know about existing social inequalities that impact decisions and could just "replicate what it has seen in the past".

While companies like Upstart and Zest AI see technology as a tool for financial inclusion, Wachter believes that real transformation requires accountability, as well as automation.

She stresses that firms must apply existing regulations and establish new guardrails to ensure that AI does not simply perpetuate the biases of the past.

"Not putting any safeguards in place will fortify, foster, and enhance discrimination."

"We've invested in creating objective, automated evaluations to identify bias, and we've incorporated those evaluations into our model training process so it evolves to become even more accurate and more fair over time," the company said in a statement.

Evangeline Atkinson (left) and Laura Mills co-founders of Noggin HQ

Evangeline Atkinson had a frustrating experience over her credit score.

"I was at university, paying rent, paying bills, as I had been doing for many years. I tried to get a Â£30-a-month phone contract, and they said no," she recalls.

"When I dug into it, it was because I was completely invisible to the system. No record could be found for me at any of the big three credit reference agencies."

That experience inspired her to try to change the system.

In 2021, Atkinson founded a credit reference agency with her best-friend from school, who had also experienced difficulties getting credit.

Their firm, Noggin HQ, was authorised by the FCA in June. Atkinson explains it was set up to help consumers access affordable credit by analysing their current account transactional data.

The platform helps lenders interpret thousands of individual data points about a person's spending habits.

Crucially, it does this without using AI, which Atkinson has also argued "carries the risk of inbuilt bias".

"You have to be very careful", she warns. "It's very sensitive data and it would be very easy for the models to be inferring various kind of protected characteristics."

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August

Perhaps conscious of change, the big three credit scoring firms have all started experimenting with AI.

Experian announced a partnership with OpenAI in August 2026, letting users check their credit score inside ChatGPT. Equifax launched a platform called Equifax Amplify AI, which it says uses machine learning to sharpen credit assessment and fraud detection.

Meanwhile, in March TransUnion announced it was integrating Google's Gemini models "within its proprietary analytic data assets".

As for the future of credit scoring, companies are starting to look beyond traditional methods, according to consumer finance lawyer Funmi Olufunwa.

"They are beginning to realise that traditional credit scoring is inherently backward-looking, and that there are better predictors of the future out there."

Whether consumers will be happy with AI playing a part in working out their future credit ratings is another thing.

Any change might be led by the younger, tech-savvy generation.

"They feel comfortable with their data being used this way, simply because it's the world they've grown up in.

"I wonder if older cohorts, who may not use technology as much, will be quite as comfortable with it," says Olufunwa.

X-planes: Are they needed in the new era of drones?

#119. Rejecting cheap fabrics, higher prices: Why shoppers are asking for better clothes

Clothing isn't made like it used to be, and more shoppers are taking notice.

The makeup of apparel imported into the U.S. has shifted in recent years. In 2010, about 60% of clothing was predominantly made from cotton. Now, that total is around 45%, while predominantly synthetic fabrics such as polyester and nylon comprise the majority of imports.

Apparel prices generally declined over the two decades leading up to the pandemic, according to the consumer price index. Since 2020, they have soared to record highs.

It's starting to change habits for consumers who are paying more for food, gas, rent and other staples. As shoppers shell out more for clothes, they're increasingly making sure the garments are worth the cost.

Consulting firm AlixPartners' 2026 Consumer Sentiment Index, which surveyed more than 9,000 shoppers, found that consumers are scrutinizing labels more closely and rejecting cheap materials sold at a premium.

"If it's not a natural material, a retailer brand who passes it off as it is, they're really becoming very low on consumers' minds," said Sonia Lapinsky, head of fashion at AlixPartners.

Fast fashion brands like Forever 21 and H&M exploded in popularity during the early 2000s and 2010s, bringing trendy clothing to shelves quickly at low prices. Then came ultra-fast fashion, with brands like Shein and Fashion Nova pumping out new garments at even quicker speeds and lower price points — made possible in part through the use of inexpensive, synthetic fabrics.

"We got to a point where the pendulum just swung too far in that direction. And we've actually begun to see a reversal," said U.S. Bancorp BTIG consumer analyst Janine Stichter. "Quality matters a lot more ... over the last three or four years than it has in the previous 10 or 20."

But quality, which shoppers often tie directly to material, can come at a higher cost.

The average unit price of apparel imported to the U.S. between January and August was $2.64 per square meter for garments that were predominantly synthetic fiber, like polyester and nylon, compared with $3.67 for items that were largely cotton, according to data from the U.S. Commerce Department's Office of Textiles and Apparel.

When comparing those 2026 figures to 2010, synthetic fiber apparel import costs have climbed around 11%, while cotton apparel costs have risen 21%.

"If you're appealing to a more value-sensitive customer, you either reduce the quality, or you negotiate with suppliers to try to share some of the cost or you maybe absorb the cost … and perhaps hope to crank a higher volume of product to make up for it," said Bernstein Research senior retail analyst Aneesha Sherman.

Though polyester is cheaper, experts point out that's not necessarily a bad thing. It can help clothing hold its shape and wick away sweat.

Sherman believes the athleisure boom is a primary factor in the shift toward polyester. She said that from 2015 to 2025, sports apparel was growing at a 4.9% rate in the U.S., versus 2.6% for all apparel and footwear, according to Euromonitor data.

"There probably is some quality trade down as well happening there," Sherman said. "But I think both these trends are conflated."

Nonetheless, backlash to synthetic materials is growing. Gen Z has even repurposed the word "polyester" as an insult to mean generally low-quality or fake.

Concerns have also grown about the environment and human health surrounding petroleum-based clothing, as more research indicates microplastics can leach from polyester clothing when washed.

It's led consumers to reexamine what materials are in their clothing.

Apparel prices have risen coming out of the pandemic, as demand outpaced supply and allowed companies to raise prices, said BTIG's Stichter. Tariffs and the Iran war followed, raising costs for businesses — and prices for consumers.

Petroleum is the basis of many synthetic fibers, and oil prices have skyrocketed since the start of the Iran war.

The same factors that helped drive up prices of clothing — Covid, tariffs and war — also affected clothing quality.

"Companies had to move around their supply chains in the post-Covid period," said Stichter. "They had to find ways to take costs out, and sometimes that meant using less expensive fabrics."

Those factors compounded a more-than-decade-long shift away from manufacturing in China.

Sheng Lu, a fashion and apparel studies professor at the University of Delaware, whose research focuses on the economics of the global textile industry, said China's established history with clothing production means it has higher-skilled workers, state-of-the-art-equipment and better material management compared with emerging manufacturing destinations such as Bangladesh, Sri Lanka and Cambodia.

"They mostly serve the value market and the mass market. And I think the reason why they're not ready to make higher-quality luxury and premium products really reflects the kind of skill sets you can expect when you source from these countries," said Lu.

AlixPartners' Lapinsky said these countries that manufacture at a lower cost sometimes have to still ship the raw materials from China, which can extend the timeline to make clothing. That means taking earlier bets on trends and then discounting if products don't sell well.

"If you have to mark down that product because the consumer doesn't want it as much, your net margin might actually be worse than if you were to just invest in quality in the first place," said BTIG's Stichter.

And as material costs rise for synthetic fibers, Stichter also said she believes it could further influence companies to use more natural fabrics moving forward.

Companies prioritizing quality are winning in this environment.

Ralph Lauren's stock is up around 85% over the past two years. Around 90% of its apparel is primarily made of cotton, leather, wool, viscose, cashmere or silk, according to its 2026 sustainability report. In its fiscal 2026, the company said it sold more products at full price and reduced markdowns.

Meanwhile, direct-to-consumer clothing brand Quince, which centers its brand identity around natural materials, reached a $10 billion valuation this year.

The brand jumped 16 spots to No. 3 for best resale value in online consignment retailer ThredUp's 2025 report. Organic cotton retailer Pact jumped 20 spots on that list to No. 8, illustrating the potential role materials are playing in brand recognition.

Alternatively, the 2026 AlixPartners report, which ranks sentiment for brands in different retail categories, found shoppers are viewing some fast fashion brands less favorably. Both Shein and Zara dropped three spots — to fifth and seventh place, respectively — in terms of sentiment in the young adult specialty category.

Shein passed higher tariff costs onto shoppers in 2025 and then blamed a sales slowdown on those higher prices. The ultra-fast fashion retailer saw its year-over-year share of U.S. apparel, accessories and footwear card spending decrease in the fourth quarter of 2025 and first two quarters of 2026, according to Consumer Edge.

Shein did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Though Zara fell in AlixPartners' Consumer Sentiment Index, it has continued to grow sales and profits year over year. Even so, Lapinsky said worsening consumer views are something to watch, even if the company is doing well.

"What we found over the years with this consumer sentiment index is that sometimes it will be a bit of a leading indicator of what's to come," said AlixPartners' Lapinsky. "I'm not saying that this is going to be true for Zara, but I think that's one thing that we'll be watching as we look in the upcoming quarters and into 2027."

#118. Anthropic’s record IPO collides with tech’s worst week on Wall Street since 2020

Startup investor Ben Narasin is so jazzed about Anthropic's blockbuster IPO that he's planning a party later this month at his Silicon Valley home. Sake, vodka and Krug Champagne will be served. Each attendee will be given two jars of caviar.

"This is a celebration of the most material, significant, and mind-blowingly awesome event that has ever happened in venture capital," Narasin, founding partner of Tenacity Venture Capital, told CNBC in an interview, adding that he didn't even invest in the company.

Anthropic raised a record $93.5 billion in its IPO and an additional $26 billion from tech giants that bought at the offer price. The stock gained 17% in its debut on Thursday, lifting Anthropic's market cap to $1.8 trillion and providing a much-needed windfall for Silicon Valley's startup ecosystem.

It was quite the split-screen moment on Wall Street, which spent the week in an AI-driven tailspin. Led by Nvidia's 23% plunge, which marked the stock's sharpest weekly pullback in 18 years, the Nasdaq slid 10% over the past five trading days in its worst week since the early days of Covid in 2020.

Investors and analysts spent the week debating whether Anthropic will trade alongside AI hardware, as it's reliant on advanced chips, memory and data centers, or whether it will stand alone as the first public pure-play AI developer and a company that's achieved unrivaled growth in less than six years of existence.

That question will likely persist for some time, but Friday's market action showed that Wall Street is skeptical of Anthropic's ability to grow into its lofty valuation. At week's end, the Claude creator's $1.6 trillion fully diluted market cap put it behind just six U.S. companies: Apple, Microsoft, Alphabet, Nvidia, Amazon and SpaceX.

With the tech market in turmoil due to rising interest rates, raging anti-AI sentiment and Nvidia's C-suite crisis, Anthropic's IPO was part exuberance, part relief for an industry hungry for exits.

After venture-backed IPOs yielded $73 billion in proceeds in 2021, the amount raised in the next four years totaled just $39 billion, according to data from University of Florida emeritus professor Jay Ritter. In 2021, 126 technology companies went public, compared with 34 in 2025.

Meanwhile, the private market has continued to balloon, with companies raising ever-larger sums at sky-high valuations without the pressures of quarterly earnings. Many startups that had hoped to go public early in the decade fell out of favor in the AI boom. They experienced hefty job cuts, down rounds and acquisitions for pennies on the dollar.

"There was a time in Silicon Valley not that long ago where the prevailing wisdom was the default path should be avoiding going public for as long as possible," said Somesh Dash, a general partner at venture firm IVP. The view was, "it's too long, it's too hard, it's not worth it," he said.

Anthropic is very young compared to some of those companies, but it was a huge collector of private capital. Investors poured $141 billion into the AI lab as of September, according to Anthropic's prospectus.

For a few Silicon Valley firms that contributed to those funding rounds, the returns are unlike anything they've ever seen. At the IPO price, Menlo Ventures and Lightspeed Venture Partners each owned about $25 billion worth of Anthropic stock. Bessemer Venture Partners owned a stake worth roughly $10 billion, partner Byron Deeter said.

Matt Murphy, a partner at Menlo, told CNBC's David Faber on Thursday that his firm has more than five limited partners "that we delivered over $1 billion of returns to."

"At the end of the day, that's really our job, is to get dollars back into foundations and endowments," Murphy said. "There's a lot of pride in being able to do that."

Bessemer's Deeter was also jubilant on Thursday, posting on X that, in addition to being "the largest bet in Bessemer's history," the IPO "is a punctuation mark in the history of both humanity and computation."

That's quite a change from a few years earlier, when Anthropic was hardly generating revenue, and success was far from guaranteed. In 2023, Anthropic's Claude 2 was struggling to keep pace with models from Google and OpenAI. In April 2024, investors at Bessemer wrote in a memo that if Anthropic missed its own targets, there was "a good chance that we lose all or part of our money."

Guru Chahal at Lightspeed told CNBC that Anthropic was clearly the team taking a more measured approach to AI. The company looked beyond Nvidia's dominant AI chips, choosing to use alternatives from Amazon and Google as well. Anthropic said it generated $1.72 in revenue per dollar spent on computing power in the third quarter, up from 68 cents a year earlier.

"It is true that it required lots of capital, but when you dig in and you realize that capital is going toward compute, and this is the team that's positioned to use that compute in the most effective way, then we could connect those dots," Chahal said. He added that those metrics showed it was "going to be a very high-quality business."

Anthropic isn't the only big win for VCs this year.

Five months before Anthropic's Nasdaq debut, SpaceX hit the market in what, until this week, was by far the largest IPO in history. SpaceX, which Elon Musk founded in 2002, produced outsized returns for firms including Sequoia and Andreessen Horowitz.

SpaceX and Anthropic alone will make 2026 a monster year for the venture industry, easily exceeding any other year in IPO proceeds. And investors in OpenAI hope appetite remains strong for trillion-dollar companies next year, as the ChatGPT maker is looking to 2027 for a potential offering.

IVP's Dash said that in his AI portfolio, "a lot of companies are starting IPO prep" even if it's only "year four, year five, year six since inception." He hopes to see at least half a dozen companies go public at valuations north of $10 billion each in the second half of 2027.

Jensen Huang's sudden departure from Nvidia has left the chip giant without a clear leader at a time when the company already faced heightened concerns about a slowdown in spending. Nvidia is a key investor in Anthropic and OpenAI and a critical supplier and financing partner for the entire AI industry, which is getting hammered across the board.

In January, Democrats will take control of the House and Senate after this month's midterm elections, which included the passage of numerous ballot measures that could stymie data center development. Anthropic investor Brad Gerstner predicted after the election that market dynamics would push Anthropic to price toward the lower end of its range.

Narasin said he wasn't even sure the company would get that far.

"I was concerned they'd delay," Narasin said.

That makes the outcome that much more impressive, he said, after Anthropic priced above its expected range and upsized the offering.

"This will be bedtime stories for years for future VCs," Narasin said. "There was a company that was created, backed by angels and later venture" that in a few short years "became the single biggest IPO of all history."

— CNBC's Ari Levy contributed to this report.

#116. Italian jeweller and businessman Paolo Bulgari dies aged 89

Italian jeweller Paolo Bulgari, who helped turn his family's business into a global luxury brand, has died in Rome aged age 89.

His family announced the news, remembering him as "the greatest jeweller of our time", according to Italian media.

Paolo Bulgari was Chairman of the Bvlgari Group since 1984 and its jewellery pieces have adorned Hollywood stars and royalty including Diana, Princess of Wales.

The company Bvlgari posted an online tribute to the "true creative soul", whose work would "continue to inspire generations to come".

The firm, Bulgari, was set up by his grandfather, Greek silversmith Sotirios Voulgaris, in Rome in the late 19th century - who later adapted his name to Bulgari in Italian.

Originally from Paramythia, a mountain village in the Epirus region of northwestern Greece renowned for its silver jewellery, Voulgaris fled Ottoman rule and settled in Naples before opening a shop in Rome.

Paolo Bulgari joined the company shortly after leaving school, becoming an expert in gemstones - eventually leading the business and overseeing international growth.

The Bulgari family favoured bold gemstones, rich yellow gold, generous proportions and unexpected colour combinations to set them apart from the delicate asethetic associated with Parisian jewellery houses.

The business broke into luxury markets and diversified beyond jewellery into watches, perfumes, leather goods and hotels.

"Paolo Bulgari began collaborating with his father in 1960, specialising in the knowledge of precious stones, of which he was one of the world's leading experts, and refining the sensibility and aesthetic sense that are at the root of the Bvlgari style," the Bvlgari company posted on social media., external

"He poured his heart into his craft and into nurturing the talents of young designers, leaving an indelible mark on the world of jewellery.

"Thank you, Mr. Paolo, your legacy as a brilliant beacon of creative inspiration will continue to inspire generations to come."

Paolo Bulgari alongside David Bowie and his wife Iman in September 1991 in Versailles, France.

The post included a quote from Paolo Bulgari: "Everything can be a source of inspiration. There are no rules in creativity. Never."

The designer brand's celebrity clients have included Elizabeth Taylor, Audrey Hepburn, Marilyn Monroe, Ingrid Bergman, Sophie Loren, Anne Hathaway, Zendaya and Rihanna.

Actor Zendaya wearing a Bvlgari necklace at the 2025 Golden Globe awards.

In 2011 the family sold its controlling stake to the French luxury group LVMH, in a deal worth about 3.7 billion euros.

In 2019, he and his wife established the Paolo Bulgari Foundation to support young people through schooling and job training in disadvantaged neighbourhoods on the outskirts of Rome.

The funeral will take place in Rome on Monday, 23 November in the Basilica of Santa Croce in Gerusalemme.

#113. Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

The prime minister made a lot of the UK's new deal with the EU cutting costs for business and consumers during tough times.

In another world, Andy Burnham would have loved for the nation's supermarkets to clearly announce that they could cut food prices as a result of this latest agreement on food exports.

And while grocers have very much welcomed Friday's announcement, they have notably not committed to price cuts as a direct result of this move.

The managing director of M&S, Alex Freudmann, called the deal a "major step forward", saying it would remove bureaucracy. Tesco chief executive Ken Murphy added it was "good news for British farmers and producers".

Freudmann highlighted some of hoops that were hurting UK-EU trade, using the example that "getting our spaghetti Bolognese from Lincolnshire onto a shelf in Dublin takes hundreds of extra miles, mountains of paperwork, a vet's signature and even the Latin name of a cow".

Such measures will no longer need to be taken once this deal kicks in. Good news for business exports then, but will that lead to lower shopping bills here in the UK?

This deal will matter for food exporters - it may not have a clear visible impact at the checkout.

Privately, industry insiders say there are too many moving parts in the world economy, especially given the energy price shock resulting from war in the Middle East.

It is more a hope than will at this stage. The industry and government hope that the reduction in costs for food producers and sellers will be passed on to customers.

This is not the first time Labour has sought to reduce the weekly shop costs. Sir Keir Starmer's administration floated the idea that supermarkets might even offer some form of branded discounts, a suggestion that was met with collective bemusement by those in the industry.

However, there will be less paperwork, checks and red tape on food imports too, but they were already lightly enforced.

No longer will hundreds of pages of documents be required. No longer will perishable goods lose valuable hours or days at border checks. No longer will the incorrect colour of ink or Latin name for a meat disallow a shipment of British exports.

Some, though not all of the 24% hit since 2019 to the real value of agrifood exports, could be regained by a bonfire of this red tape.

In particular, smaller exporters of food will be able to return to the large EU market.

But the extra benefit here is unclear, and there could be extra checks and costs required instead on non-EU food imports.

The government's own assessment is that the economy will gain Â£5bn through this, or 0.14% by 2040.

Critics point out that this benefit does not include the quid pro quo - having to align with EU laws. Farmers have pointed to compliance with pesticides and fungicide regulation.

The classic example of the benefits of post-Brexit autonomy has been the development of precision-bred foodstuffs, such as Vitamin D-enhanced tomatoes.

The producer of this technology has, however, backed the deal after the government negotiated an "exception period" for ongoing UK sale.

Professor Cristobal Uauy of the John Innes Centre said that "aligning with the EU market will bring our science to a greater number of people".

Bringing all the agreements on food, emissions and the youth experience together, the economy is forecast to get an overall boost of 0.5%, or Â£17bn a year.

A modest boost, but high by the standards of past post-Brexit trade deals, for example, with Australia, India or Japan and the Gulf and the trans-pacific deal.

This UK-EU pact actually matches all of those agreements combined for expected growth measured by the Treasury's own assessments.

The biggest single boost to the overall economy would come from more European young workers. However, the economy benefit of this part of the deal per person reduces to zero, according to the government's own calculations.

Burham also said he hoped a planned electricity deal with the EU would cut bills, but that has not been done yet, and requires a contribution into the bloc's budget for poorer regions.

The electricity deal could help lower bills, but that contains a clear trade-off.

There are still some details to be revealed in the full legal texts of these documents.

The bigger point is whether this logic of gaining free-flowing access for food, in exchange for giving up regulatory freedom, will now apply elsewhere.

Is food now a blueprint for future negotiations? It had been the Starmer-Rachel Reeves strategy to spread this model to other sectors such as cars, and chemicals.

The big takeaway from today was that both the PM and his counterparts publicly acknowledged the end of the incremental piecemeal approach to aligning with the single market.

The question is whether the PM thinks what goes for food applies across the whole economy. Most economists predict that could lead to much bigger economic gains, but it will also be a much bigger constitutional battle.

#111. Can AI really help spot Black Friday deals? We put it to the test

Whether it's scrolling through endless choice, spotting fake reviews, or working out if a "deal" is a deal at all, Black Friday sums up a lot of online woes.

Retailers promise bargains - but the sheer number of offers can leave us feeling no closer to finding the best deal.

It's an area where generative AI products, such as ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini, have promised to help.

But are they any good at finding Black Friday deals? We had a go ourselves a week ahead of the big day.

Figures suggest relatively few UK shoppers currently go to AI tools for help.

A recent YouGov survey of 19 countries found the UK ranked second-last in the proportion of people who said they had used AI to help them shop.

Crucially, 36% of respondents who said they would be shopping around Black Friday didn't plan to use an AI tool at all.

But the survey suggested a growing number of people were considering it - and tech firms have released features to capitalise on this.

Amazon launched Alexa for Shopping for UK users in November and OpenAI launched a Shopping Research tool on ChatGPT days before last year's Black Friday.

In the US, Google recently launched Universal Cart, with AI-powered features such as price tracking and identification of relevant promo codes.

But there has been criticism that fact-checking AI ends up taking more time, while some brands are working to try to make sure their products show up in chatbot answers.

We asked four models to find a 55-inch TV deal, using the free versions of many of the most popular tools.

While this was not a rigorous scientific test, the experience we had produced mixed results.

OpenAI's ChatGPT came up with four options.

Its top pick was a Hisense TV on Chinese retailer Joybuy's website, on sale for Â£499 down from Â£649.

The next recommendation was a 55-inch TCL-branded TV, but a sidebar showed prices for the 50-inch version, from Â£379 to Â£814.

Option three was the same Hisense TV as before, but for full price at Â£649 from John Lewis and Richer Sounds.

This was the "best safer purchase" according to ChatGPT, as the sites offered longer guarantee periods.

But clicking on the links did not go well. Both retailers said the TV was no longer listed online.

The fourth option was equally fruitless - another TCL TV, at full price, Â£150 over our budget and not available online.

At the bottom, ChatGPT served one last offer - "one OLED option to compare" - for a Â£1,100 LG TV that was marked as "Ad".

Claude produced the shortest answer of all the models we tested, and unlike the others, it did not give much detail on the specifications of each model.

Instead, it seemed to base its results on a small number of reviews published online.

It recommended the same two TCL TVs as ChatGPT.

But when we clicked on the links, they sent us to two dead pages on the Richer Sounds website.

Some other links led to year-old round-ups of Black Friday 2025 deals.

Claude was not very enthusiastic about its other two recommendations due to a lack of independent reviews - calling one "a decent option" but saying it "wouldn't pick" the other.

It also listed two Samsung TVs, but it told us not to buy them, with one "over budget and not clearly better than the TCLs".

We were left wondering why it even mentioned them in the first place.

Different AI model, same TV manufacturers - albeit different Hisense and TCL models to the previous chatbots' recommendations.

Gemini generated a table comparing four different models, but offered no links to the stores it named, so we searched for them ourselves.

The prices for one TCL TV were correct at Â£649, but it was sold out at one of the named retailers.

It said the other TCL model was available from Argos and Currys for Â£539 - correct for Argos, but when we checked Currys, it was Â£60 more expensive.

Again, it included two Samsung models to avoid due to lower screen specs.

Alexa came back with four TVs, from its own Amazon Ember at Â£532 to a Â£699 Samsung.

But its top recommendation was to go for another Samsung model at Â£616 (down from Â£749), based on its picture quality and Amazon customer rating.

Its fourth option was for a Sony TV, but it was only 50 inches, leaving us unsure why it had included it.

All the prices stated were correct, but Amazon only searches its own store. Alexa did acknowledge this, encouraging us to check its rivals for better deals, "as prices can vary" and we could get longer guarantees elsewhere.

There are methods that can boost the likelihood of getting a better response, from crafting more informative prompts to upping a chatbot's "thinking" level.

Tech journalist Alex Hughes had better luck than we did when he used ChatGPT's agentic AI mode to find a TV in last year's Black Friday sales.

Agentic AI is still in its early days, but it allows AI to act with more autonomy and carry out multi-step tasks.

The search took about 20 minutes "with very little work on my end needed," Hughes wrote for Tom's Guide, external in November 2025.

A year on, he told the BBC he didn't go through with buying a TV at that time.

He did later purchase one of the seven models ChatGPT had surfaced - but he had found it for a lower price in a bricks-and-mortar shop.

Will he be using AI to find Black Friday deals again this year? "Probably not," he said.

He reckoned it saved him time, but "when you're trying to deal with something like live price data... it will be inconsistent and quite often incorrect".

While we were served up the same brands again and again, in reality there are tonnes of products listed for sale around Black Friday.

Naureen Mohammed, who advises businesses on how to get their products to appear in AI results, told the BBC customers did not see the full picture when they talked to chatbots.

If businesses fail to update their prices when people start hunting for bargains, "last year's prices are served up as today's deals".

"I suspect the whole model for advertising is going to start influencing AI recommendations," she said.

Her own analysis of chatbots' Black Friday tech deals in the UK suggested the big retailers came out on top - with Amazon mentioned in half of responses, and Currys following closely behind.

Her tips include asking an assistant "is that this year's price?" and clicking on the links provided - "because you shouldn't take any advice from the AI system at face value".

But consumer group Which? recently said inaccurate pricing in AI results added more work, external to Black Friday deal-hunting.

"If you ask ChatGPT for a list of 10 laptops under Â£400 and seven of the results are priced higher, you've likely wasted a good chunk of time going through them to discover the bot gave you only three options," said senior researcher Hannah Walsh.

"The opposite is also true - you could miss out on great models in your budget if ChatGPT tells you they are more expensive than they actually are."

Avoid this Black Friday scam - how to check it's a real deal

Listen: Black Friday - are you really saving money?

#110. Quantum computing’s first real-world payoff may come in energy, not drug discovery

For years, the case for quantum computing has rested on ambitious promises, from discovering new drugs by simulating how molecules behave to optimizing supply chains and airline schedules. Many of the biggest aims are still likely to be years, if not decades, away.

But quantum software, which uses algorithms designed to solve complex problems faster than conventional computers, is already being tested on a more immediate challenge: the energy transition, from keeping power grids stable to finding better materials for solar cells and hydrogen production.

Phasecraft, a quantum software company founded in Bristol, England, in 2019, is one of the firms putting the technology to work in energy. It raised $34 million in Series B funding in 2025, and in September this year announced plans to set up U.S. headquarters in Arlington, Virginia.

There are enormous opportunities to solve problems that are both important for energy security and commercially valuable, Ashley Montanaro, co-founder and CEO of Phasecraft, told CNBC.

Phasecraft isn't alone in applying quantum software to the energy industry.

Quantinuum, which was formed by the merger of Cambridge Quantum and Honeywell Quantum Solutions and listed on Nasdaq in June, is using the technology to model materials for hydrogen fuel cells for transportation. It is also working with partners on materials for carbon capture.

Another U.K. player, ORCA Computing, a spinout from the University of Oxford, has partnered with BP to better understand how a molecule's physical, chemical and biological properties are governed. That could help explain how biofuels burn and how to make them burn more efficiently

In a separate project, ORCA worked with Japanese software company JIJ on a challenge power companies face: deciding which generators to switch on or off, and when, to meet demand at the lowest cost.

That project also involved BP and the National Quantum Computing Centre (NQCC), the U.K.'s national laboratory for quantum computing, which is part of the government-backed U.K. Research and Innovation. The involvement of the NQCC, like that of ARPA-E, suggests governments are paying close attention to quantum software's potential role in energy, as are companies such as BP.

For Phasecraft, a priority is grid stability and optimization. It has partnered with the National Energy System Operator (NESO), the independent system planner that balances energy supply and demand in England, Scotland and Wales, on grid optimization tasks, including managing power exchanges and isolating network sections during emergencies. The company says its software can complete these tasks up to 1,000 times faster than conventional computing.

Phasecraft believes its grid software could be particularly sought after in Asia, where electrification is rapidly expanding, increasing the need for grid resilience to balance high and growing demand with the intermittent supply offered by renewables.

The company is also working with U.K. solar cell maker Oxford PV on the development of new materials for photovoltaic cells capable of operating more efficiently than existing ones.

Separately, it has teamed up with London-listed specialty materials developer Johnson Matthey, Harvard University and Boston-based quantum hardware specialist QuEra, in work overseen by the U.S. government's Advanced Research Projects Agency–Energy (ARPA-E), to discover novel catalysts that would reduce reliance on scarce metals like iridium — which is currently critical in the production of hydrogen.

Asked why Phasecraft is focusing on energy rather than sectors that might seem more lucrative, Montanaro said the returns were there too.

"When you get this right, the commercial returns are real: more efficient energy networks mean lower operating costs and more value from existing infrastructure," he said.
